\section{Results and precise conventions}\label{ccc:dc:sec:intro}

\wsevthree\ ``The pinned report'', ``the source report'' and ``the report''
of this Part are this report as it stood at the pin, Parts~I--III; ``the
preceding quadratic NFA note'' is the manuscript of Part~IV, and ``the
companion note'' of Section~\ref{ccc:dc:sec:leading-one} the manuscript of
Part~V. The manuscript's citations of all three are replaced by internal
references.

The pinned report asks for the exact complexity of deciding finite stabilization for complete DFAs over a fixed binary alphabet (Part~II, Question~\ref{ccc:ps:q:dfa}). Its NFA result is $\mathsf{PSPACE}$-complete; it leaves the deterministic case open. The first result below classifies that case. The second resolves the related extremal-rank order for complete binary DFAs, which remained open in the preceding quadratic NFA note (Part~IV, Question~\ref{ccc:qr:q:dfa}).

\begin{theorem}[Decision complexity]\label{ccc:dc:thm:complexity}
Given an explicitly represented complete binary DFA, deciding whether its language has finite constrained self-crossover stabilization is $\coNP$-complete under polynomial-time many-one reductions. This holds for both parallel and frozen-source iterations. Nonstabilization is $\NP$-complete. The $\coNP$ upper bound extends to every explicitly given finite alphabet.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Quadratic finite rank with sharp leading constant]\label{ccc:dc:thm:rank}
For every $m\ge2$, there is a complete binary DFA with one initial state and exactly $m+6$ states whose language $\Ldfa m$ satisfies
\[
(m-1)^2+2\le R(\Ldfa m)\le(m-1)^2+4<\infty.
\]
Consequently, if $F_{\rm DFA}(s)$ denotes the maximum finite $R(L)$ among complete binary DFAs with at most $s$ states, then
\[
(s-7)^2+2\le F_{\rm DFA}(s)\le s^2+3\qquad(s\ge8).
\]
In particular, $F_{\rm DFA}(s)/s^2\to1$ as $s\to\infty$.
\end{theorem}

The lower witness in Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:rank} is not asserted to attain the constructed language's maximum rank. The stated two-unit interval is intentional. All state counts include the initial selector, accepting monochromatic states, and rejecting sink.

A complete DFA is $\mathcal A=(Q,\Sigma,\delta,q_{\rm in},F)$, where $|Q|=s$, $\delta:Q\times\Sigma\to Q$ is total, and there are no $\eps$-transitions. Our lower bounds use $\Sigma=\{0,1\}$. All complexity claims concern the original explicit DFA, rather than an already expanded periodic graph.

\section{Rank and the inherited periodic criteria}\label{ccc:dc:sec:background}

For equal-length words $x,y\in\Sigma^n$, aligned one-point crossover at $0\le i\le n$ yields either $x[0:i]y[i:n]$ or $y[0:i]x[i:n]$. For languages $A,B$, let $A\cut B$ contain all such outputs with one parent from each language. Endpoint cuts are allowed. The two iterations are
\[
L^{[0]}=L,\quad L^{[k+1]}=L^{[k]}\cut L^{[k]},
\qquad Y_0=L,\quad Y_{k+1}=Y_k\cut L.
\]

For $w\in\Sigma^n$, $n>0$, a nonempty interval $[i,j)$ is admissible if a seed $v\in L\cap\Sigma^n$ agrees with $w$ on that interval. Rank $\rank_L(w)$ is the minimum number of such intervals partitioning the target; it is infinite if no partition exists. The coordinate hull $\rec(L)$ consists of the finite-rank words, with $\eps$ included exactly when it is a seed. Set $\rank_L(\eps)=1$ if accepted and infinity otherwise, and
\[
R(L)=\sup\bigl(\{1\}\cup\{\rank_L(w):w\in\rec(L)\}\bigr).
\]
The report's established fragment formulas are
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:dc:eq:clocks}
L^{[k]}=\{w:\rank_L(w)\le2^k\},\qquad
Y_k=\{w:\rank_L(w)\le k+1\}.
\end{equation}
Thus either iteration stabilizes finitely exactly when $R(L)<\infty$; the least indices are $\lceil\log_2R(L)\rceil$ and $R(L)-1$. These are credited background (Part~I's Theorems~\ref{ccc:thm:generation} and~\ref{ccc:thm:frozen} and Corollary~\ref{ccc:cor:stages}; Part~II's Proposition~\ref{ccc:ps:prop:rank-clock} and Corollary~\ref{ccc:ps:cor:bounded}; Part~IV's Proposition~\ref{ccc:qr:prop:clocks}), not new claims of this note.
\wsevthree\ This section restates the report's definitions in the
manuscript's words; it defines nothing new. Formula~\eqref{ccc:dc:eq:clocks}
is Part~II's~\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:clocks}.

Let $E$ be the unlabelled transition relation and define
\[
P_i=E^i(\{q_{\rm in}\}),\qquad
R_j=\{q:\exists f\in F,\ (q,f)\in E^j\}.
\]
The source report supplies the common transient and period
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:dc:eq:constants}
t=2(s+2)^2+2(s+2),\qquad p=\operatorname{lcm}(1,2,\ldots,s+2),
\end{equation}
with $P_{i+p}=P_i$ and $R_{i+p}=R_i$ for $i\ge t$. It derives this polynomial transient from corrected unary automata results; the source dependencies are recorded there. What matters here is that $t$ is polynomial, $\log p=O(s\log(s+2))$, and every cyclic SCC period divides $p$.
\wsevthree\ These are Part~II's constants $T_s$ and $p_s$
of~\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:universal-constants} (Lemma~\ref{ccc:ps:lem:quadratic-period},
from To's corrected unary theorem~\cite{To}); the sets $P_i$, $R_j$, $S_{r,a}$,
$C_{r,a}$ below are Part~II's \eqref{ccc:ps:eq:PR},
\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:stable-states} and~\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:stable-letters} for a
DFA, and the safe family and safe core are
Definition~\ref{ccc:ps:def:safe-core}.

For a phase $a\bmod p$, write $P^\infty_a=P_i$ and $R^\infty_a=R_i$ for any $i\ge t$ in that phase. A total-length residue $r$ is \emph{live} if $P^\infty_r\cap F\ne\varnothing$. For live $r$, set
\begin{align}
S_{r,a}&=P^\infty_a\cap R^\infty_{r-a},\label{ccc:dc:eq:S}\\
C_{r,a}&=\{c\in\Sigma:\exists q\in S_{r,a},\ \delta(q,c)\in S_{r,a+1}\}.
\label{ccc:dc:eq:C}
\end{align}
All these viable state sets and coordinate alphabets are nonempty. The labelled phase graph has vertices $(a,q)$ for $q\in S_{r,a}$; a permitted letter $c\in C_{r,a}$ advances to $(a+1,\delta(q,c))$ if that vertex is viable. A word is \emph{phase-admissible} when each letter belongs to the corresponding $C_{r,a}$; it is \emph{liftable} if some viable starting state reads it without leaving this graph.

A \emph{safe family} is a family $U_a\subseteq S_{r,a}$ closed under every permitted letter:
\[
q\in U_a,\ c\in C_{r,a}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\delta(q,c)\in U_{a+1}.
\]
The greatest such family is the \emph{safe core}. The inherited results used for the complexity proof are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Finite stabilization holds exactly when every live residue has no phase-admissible nonliftable word.
\item For a DFA, a live residue has no such obstruction exactly when its safe core is nonempty. A nonempty safe core meets every phase.
\end{enumerate}
These are the report's \emph{Interior universality criterion} and \emph{Safe-core characterization} (Part~II's Theorems~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:criterion} and~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:core}). To recall why determinism matters in the second statement: if the safe core is empty, every vertex has a permitted continuation that leaves the viable graph. Append a killing continuation for one currently surviving state. Each other state has at most one image, so the surviving-image count drops. After at most $s$ rounds no starting state survives, producing an obstruction. Conversely, a safe vertex at every phase reads every permitted word.
\wsevthree\ This recalls Part~II's proof of Theorem~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:core}; it is
not a second route.

For polynomial-time tests of the stable sets, use the representative
\[
i_a=t+((a-t)\bmod p).
\]
Boolean matrix powering computes $E^{i_a}$ in polynomial time in $s$ and $\log i_a$. Every phase test below uses only polynomially many such operations, without expanding all $p$ phases.

\section{Compressing safe cores into SCC stripes}\label{ccc:dc:sec:stripes}

A \emph{cyclic SCC} is a strongly connected component containing a directed cycle, including a singleton with a self-loop. For each such component $H$, let $d_H$ be the gcd of its closed-walk lengths. Fix a reference vertex $z_H$ and a cyclic-class function
\[
\kappa_H:H\to\mathbb Z/d_H\mathbb Z,\qquad\kappa_H(z_H)=0,
\]
so that every internal edge $u\to v$ satisfies $\kappa_H(v)=\kappa_H(u)+1$. These data are computable in polynomial time from directed distances and the gcd of the edge discrepancies $\operatorname{dist}(u)+1-\operatorname{dist}(v)$. We have $d_H\le|H|$, hence
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:dc:eq:stripe-count}
\sum_{H\ \mathrm{cyclic}}d_H\le s.
\end{equation}
In particular $d_H\mid p$ for the period in \eqref{ccc:dc:eq:constants}.
\wsevthree\ The delivered source of the last display lost a \verb|\rm| at a
line break and printed the subscript as ``$H\ mcyclic$''; it is printed as
intended, the sum over the cyclic SCCs.

For $h\in\mathbb Z/d_H\mathbb Z$, define a \emph{stripe}
\[
W(H,h)=\{(a,q):q\in H,\ a-\kappa_H(q)\equiv h\pmod{d_H}\},
\]
where $a$ ranges over phases modulo $p$. Although a stripe can contain exponentially many phase vertices, it has a polynomial-size name $(H,h)$.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform viability]\label{ccc:dc:lem:viability}
For fixed live $r$, every stripe is either wholly viable or wholly nonviable. The stripe $W(H,h)$ is viable exactly when $z_H\in S_{r,h}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $d=d_H$. Closed-walk lengths at any $q\in H$ contain every sufficiently large multiple of $d$. Indeed, a finite collection of such lengths already has gcd $d$, and its additive semigroup contains all sufficiently large multiples of $d$; concatenating the corresponding walks realizes them. The finite gcd fact follows, after dividing by $d$, by choosing one generator $b$ and a representable value in every residue modulo $b$, then adding sufficiently many copies of $b$.

For every attained residue modulo $d$ of a path from $q_{\rm in}$ to $q$, fix one witnessing path and append the long closed walks at $q$. Hence forward membership at $q$ is eventually $d$-periodic. Prepending these closed walks to witnesses from $q$ to a final state proves the analogous fact for backward membership. Stable values defined with the larger period $p$ therefore factor through residues modulo $d$: evaluate them at arbitrarily large representatives of each $p$-phase. This does not require the smaller period's transient to equal the chosen $t$.

For $q,v\in H$, choose internal paths $q\to v$ of length $\ell$ and $v\to q$ of length $k$. Then $\ell+k\equiv0\pmod d$ and $\ell\equiv\kappa_H(v)-\kappa_H(q)\pmod d$. Concatenating the paths and using eventual $d$-periodicity gives
\[
q\in P^\infty_a\iff v\in P^\infty_{a+\ell},\qquad
q\in R^\infty_{r-a}\iff v\in R^\infty_{r-a-\ell}.
\]
Their intersection makes viability constant throughout a stripe. Taking $q=z_H$ and phase $h$ gives the claimed test.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Internal edges are permitted]\label{ccc:dc:lem:edges}
If $(a,q)$ belongs to a viable stripe in $H$ and $\delta(q,c)=v\in H$, then $v\in S_{r,a+1}$ and $c\in C_{r,a}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The edge advances the cyclic class by one, so its endpoints belong to the same stripe. Apply Lemma~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:viability} and \eqref{ccc:dc:eq:C}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[One safe vertex forces its stripe]\label{ccc:dc:lem:propagation}
If a safe family contains one vertex in a cyclic SCC $H$, it contains that vertex's entire stripe.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Lemma~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:edges} and safety force every internal labelled path from that vertex to remain in the family, with the corresponding phase advance. Paths from $q$ to $v$ inside $H$ exist at all sufficiently large lengths congruent to $\kappa_H(v)-\kappa_H(q)$ modulo $d_H$: take one fixed path and insert sufficiently long closed walks at its start. For any desired compatible target phase $b$, choose such a length congruent to $b-a$ modulo $p$. This is possible because $d_H\mid p$. Every vertex of the stripe is therefore reached.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Stripe criterion]\label{ccc:dc:thm:stripe}
For a live residue $r$, the safe core is nonempty if and only if some stripe $W(H,h)$ is viable and has no permitted exit from its original SCC:
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:dc:eq:noexit}
(a,q)\in W(H,h),\ c\in C_{r,a}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\delta(q,c)\in H.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For sufficiency, a viable stripe satisfying \eqref{ccc:dc:eq:noexit} is itself safe: every internal edge advances the cyclic class and stays in that stripe.

For necessity, consider the original SCCs represented by vertices of a nonempty safe core. Choose a sink among these represented SCCs in the condensation partial order. This need not be a sink SCC of the whole automaton. Every represented phase vertex has a permitted successor, because $C_{r,a}$ is nonempty; safety puts that successor in the core. The sink choice keeps it in the selected component $H$. Thus $H$ is cyclic, since a singleton without a self-loop could not have such a successor. Lemma~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:propagation} forces an entire stripe into the core. A permitted exit from that stripe would, by safety, lead to a strictly lower represented SCC, contradicting the sink choice.
\end{proof}

The qualification concerning represented SCCs is essential. For example, an accepting $0$-loop in a DFA for $0^*$ can be safe although its forbidden $1$-edge leads to a rejecting bottom component. Safety concerns permitted letters, not all letters at every phase.

\section{A polynomial certificate for nonstabilization}\label{ccc:dc:sec:upper}

\begin{proposition}\label{ccc:dc:prop:conp}
Finite stabilization for complete DFAs over an explicitly given finite alphabet belongs to $\coNP$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A certificate for nonstabilization consists of a live residue $r<p$ and, for every viable stripe $W(H,h)$, a triple $(q,a,c)$ satisfying
\begin{align*}
&q\in H,\qquad a<p,\qquad a-\kappa_H(q)\equiv h\pmod{d_H},\\
&c\in C_{r,a},\qquad \delta(q,c)\notin H.
\end{align*}
The verifier computes the cyclic SCCs, their periods and cyclic classes, and all stripe names. It checks that $r$ is live. For each stripe it deterministically tests viability using $z_H\in S_{r,h}$. A nonviable stripe requires no exit witness; for each viable one, it verifies the displayed conditions.

There are at most $s$ stripes by \eqref{ccc:dc:eq:stripe-count}. Every guessed phase uses $O(\log p)$ bits, polynomial in the input. Stable membership and permitted-letter predicates are computable by polynomially many Boolean matrix powers at polynomial-bit exponents. All other checks are elementary graph and modular arithmetic. Therefore certificate size and verification time are polynomial.

The stripe criterion says such exit witnesses exist for exactly those live residues with empty safe core. The inherited criteria in Section~\ref{ccc:dc:sec:background} say that at least one such residue exists exactly when stabilization fails. Hence nonstabilization is in $\NP$, and stabilization is in $\coNP$.
\end{proof}

This certificate avoids expanding the full phase graph. An exit may lead to another viable or even safe region; that does not invalidate the criterion. If any safe region existed, a lowest represented SCC would supply some stripe with no permitted exit, and the certificate would fail for that stripe.

\section{Binary DFA hardness via length testers}\label{ccc:dc:sec:hardness}

The arithmetic coding in this reduction is classical. Prime-residue assignment encodings and invalid-assignment and clause-falsification testers appear explicitly in Stockmeyer and Meyer's proof of Theorem~6.1 \cite{SM}. Closely related DFA length constructions, including binary selectors feeding unary cycles, appear in Gawrychowski et al.\ \cite[Theorems~5--6]{GLRSS}. We credit those ingredients. The crossover-specific step is the monochromatic/cylinder embedding below, which turns uncovered lengths into high aligned rank.
\wsevthree\ Part~III's Theorem~\ref{ccc:cl:thm:complexity} is also a
coNP-completeness result, but for thresholding the exact depth of a succinct
list of binary-encoded rays, by a CLIQUE reduction; Part~VI decides whether
stabilization happens at all for an explicit DFA. The two concern different
inputs and problems, and neither implies the other. The manuscript does not
cite Part~III.

\subsection{A polynomial family of unary testers}

Start with a 3CNF formula $\varphi$ in variables $x_1,\ldots,x_N$. Remove tautological clauses and repeated literals inside a clause. Add an unused variable when necessary so that $N\ge1$. Choose distinct primes $p_1,\ldots,p_N$, each polynomially bounded in $N$. The first $N$ primes suffice: their largest value is $O(N\log(N+1))$, and a sieve constructs them in polynomial time.

A length $n$ encodes a Boolean assignment when $n\bmod p_i\in\{0,1\}$ for every $i$, with residue one meaning true. Use the following cycle testers:
\begin{itemize}
\item For variable $i$, a cycle of modulus $p_i$ marks the residues outside $\{0,1\}$.
\item For each clause, a cycle whose modulus is the product of its at most three distinct variable primes marks the residue that makes every literal of the clause false. An empty clause uses modulus one and marks every length.
\end{itemize}
The Chinese remainder theorem determines the clause residue. Call a tester \emph{active} at $n$ if it marks that total length. If $\varphi$ is unsatisfiable, every length activates some tester: either its residues do not encode an assignment or they encode an assignment falsifying a clause. If $\varphi$ is satisfiable, its satisfying assignment gives an infinite arithmetic progression of lengths activating none, modulo $\prod_i p_i$.

Let $K=N+\#\{\text{clauses}\}\ge1$ and $B=\max_i p_i$. The cycles use at most $KB^3$ states. Their active-residue sets can be computed by enumerating their polynomially many residues. This construction is polynomial in the formula size. A variable tester with modulus two simply has no active residue, which causes no problem.

\subsection{A deterministic binary selector with fragile constant branches}

Build a complete binary DFA whose first two letters select one of four cases:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cl}
\toprule
Prefix & Destination\\
\midrule
$00$ & Accepting state with a $0$-loop; letter $1$ rejects forever\\
$11$ & Accepting state with a $1$-loop; letter $0$ rejects forever\\
$01$ & Fixed-depth binary selector for the $K$ testers\\
$10$ & Rejecting sink\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The selector uses $d=\lceil\log_2 K\rceil$ additional bits, with unused codes going to the sink. All tester entries are at the same total depth
\[
D=2+d.
\]
For $K=1$, no extra selector bits or decision nodes are needed. Every tester is a directed cycle on which both input letters advance one step. If its modulus is $h$ and its desired set of active total-length residues is $A\subseteq\mathbb Z/h\mathbb Z$, declare cycle position $j$ final exactly when
\[
D+j\pmod h\in A.
\]
This explicit offset ensures that activity depends on total word length, including the selector. Prefix and selector states are nonfinal, and all unspecified transitions go to a rejecting sink with both loops. The selector has $O(K)$ states, so the whole DFA remains polynomial in size.

The only seeds accepted outside tester branches are $0^n$ and $1^n$ for $n\ge2$. If a tester is active at $n\ge D$, its fixed selector prefix can be followed by \emph{every} binary suffix of length $n-D$ to obtain a seed. Thus it contributes a full suffix cylinder.

\begin{lemma}[Correctness of the crossover embedding]\label{ccc:dc:lem:reduction}
The constructed DFA stabilizes finitely if and only if $\varphi$ is unsatisfiable.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $\varphi$ is satisfiable, there are arbitrarily large $n\ge D$ with no active tester. At each such length the entire seed slice is $\{0^n,1^n\}$. Both symbols occur at every coordinate, so the hull slice is all binary words, but admissible fragments are exactly constant intervals. An alternating target has rank $n$. Hence $R(L)=\infty$ and no finite stabilization occurs.

If $\varphi$ is unsatisfiable, some tester is active at every length. For $n>D$ and any hull target $w$, its first $D$ letters can be supplied as singleton fragments using $0^n$ and $1^n$. The remaining suffix is one admissible aligned fragment supplied by an active tester cylinder. Thus $\rank_L(w)\le D+1$. At $n=D$, omit the empty suffix, obtaining rank at most $D$. At smaller positive lengths, the singleton bound on existing hull slices gives rank at most $n<D$. The length-zero convention is harmless. Therefore $R(L)\le D+1<\infty$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:complexity}]
The map from $\varphi$ to its complete binary DFA is polynomial and sends unsatisfiable 3CNF formulas exactly to finitely stabilizing languages. This gives $\coNP$-hardness. Proposition~\ref{ccc:dc:prop:conp} gives membership. Taking complements yields $\NP$-completeness of nonstabilization. Formula~\eqref{ccc:dc:eq:clocks} makes the two iteration clocks equivalent for this decision problem.
\end{proof}

\section{A deterministic family with quadratic finite rank}\label{ccc:dc:sec:family}

The rank construction is independent of the complexity reduction. It reuses the classical two-cycle graph underlying the earlier NFA family, while assigning different letters to its two branching edges and using a deterministic selector.
\wsevthree\ The earlier NFA family is Part~IV's gate language $\Lgate m$
(Section~\ref{ccc:qr:sec:gate}). Below, $S$, $H$ and $g$ are Part~IV's $S_m$,
$H_m$ and $g_m$ with the index dropped, and the first two assertions of
Lemma~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:gate} are Part~IV's Lemmas~\ref{ccc:qr:lem:returns}
and~\ref{ccc:qr:lem:gap} (Sylvester's two-generator Frobenius formula shifted
by $m$; see the note after Lemma~\ref{ccc:qr:lem:gap}), proved again directly;
the common completion length is new.

\subsection{The complete automaton}
Fix $m\ge2$ and use gate states $q_0,\ldots,q_{m-1}$ with
\[
\delta(q_i,0)=\delta(q_i,1)=q_{i+1}\quad(0\le i<m-1),
\]
\[
\delta(q_{m-1},0)=q_0,\qquad\delta(q_{m-1},1)=q_1.
\]
Only $q_0$ is final inside the gate. Add exactly six states: an initial state $q_{\rm in}$, prefix states $a,b$, accepting monochromatic states $z,o$, and a rejecting sink $d$. Their transitions are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
State & $0$-successor & $1$-successor\\
\midrule
$q_{\rm in}$ & $a$ & $b$\\
$a$ & $z$ & $q_0$\\
$b$ & $d$ & $o$\\
$z$ & $z$ & $d$\\
$o$ & $d$ & $o$\\
$d$ & $d$ & $d$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The final set is $\{q_0,z,o\}$ and $q_{\rm in}$ is the sole initial state.
\wsevthree\ The manuscript names the initial state $r$; it is printed
$q_{\rm in}$, the manuscript's own name for an initial state in
Section~\ref{ccc:dc:sec:intro}, because $r$ denotes a residue elsewhere. This is a complete binary DFA with exactly $m+6$ states. Prefix $01$ enters the gate; outside that prefix, the seeds are exactly $0^n,1^n$ for $n\ge2$. In particular the hull consists of every binary word of length at least two; its length-zero and length-one slices are empty.

\subsection{The quadratic missing return length}
Put
\[
S=\langle m,m-1\rangle=\{am+b(m-1):a,b\in\N\},
\quad H=\{0\}\cup(m+S),\quad g=(m-1)^2.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Gate lengths and common completion]\label{ccc:dc:lem:gate}
The lengths of paths from $q_0$ to itself are exactly $H$, whose largest missing nonnegative integer is $g$. From every gate state there is a path to $q_0$ of length exactly $g+1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The unlabelled gate graph has an $m$-cycle and an $(m-1)$-cycle sharing a path. A positive first return to $q_0$ has length $m+b(m-1)$, with $b\ge0$. Concatenating first returns gives exactly $am+b(m-1)$ with $a\ge1$, hence the set $m+S$. Assigning different labels to the two exits from $q_{m-1}$ changes which words label these paths, but not which path lengths exist.

To see that $g\notin H$, an expression $g=m+xm+y(m-1)$ would imply $x\equiv m-2\pmod{m-1}$ and therefore $m+xm\ge m(m-1)>g$. This includes $m=2$, where the modulus is one. If $N\ge g+1$, divide $N-m=q(m-1)+r$, with $0\le r\le m-2$. Then $q\ge m-2\ge r$, so
\[
N=m+rm+(q-r)(m-1)\in H.
\]
Thus $g$ is the last missing return length.

The semigroup $S$ contains every integer at least $(m-1)(m-2)$: write such an integer as $q(m-1)+r$, $0\le r\le m-2$, and use $rm+(q-r)(m-1)$. For $i\ge1$, the path lengths from $q_i$ to $q_0$ are exactly $(m-i)+S$, by the same first-return decomposition. With $B=g+1$,
\[
B-(m-i)=(m-1)(m-2)+i\in S.
\]
For $i=0$, $B\in H$ was already shown. Hence every gate state reaches $q_0$ in exactly $B$ steps.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Witness and direct finite bound]\label{ccc:dc:prop:family}
The constructed language satisfies $g+2\le R(\Ldfa m)\le g+4$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
At total length $n=g+2$, a word beginning with $01$ would require a gate return of length $g$, which is impossible. Hence the full seed slice is exactly $\{0^n,1^n\}$. An alternating target lies in the hull and every admissible fragment is constant, giving rank exactly $n=g+2$.

For the upper bound, put $B=g+1$. Let $w$ have length $n>B+2$. Its central interval $w[2:n-B]$ is nonempty. Read this central word from $q_0$; completeness keeps its unique run inside the gate. Lemma~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:gate} supplies a completion of length $B$ from its last state to $q_0$. Prefixing $01$ produces an accepted length-$n$ seed agreeing with the whole central interval at its proper coordinates. The first two target letters and last $B$ target letters are supplied as singleton fragments by the monochromatic seeds. Consequently
\[
\rank_{\Ldfa m}(w)\le2+1+B=g+4.
\]
For $2\le n\le B+2$, singletons give rank at most $n\le g+3$. The two shorter hull slices are empty and the baseline value one is below $g+4$. This proves the uniform finite bound without using a safe-core or complexity theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:rank}]
Proposition~\ref{ccc:dc:prop:family} gives the stated family and its finiteness. Set $m=s-6$ for $s\ge8$ to obtain $F_{\rm DFA}(s)\ge(s-7)^2+2$. The complete-DFA upper bound $R(L)\le s^2+3$ is proved in Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:leading-one} below. Together the inequalities give the asserted bound and, after division by $s^2$, the limit one.
\end{proof}

The largest finite parallel stabilization index for complete binary DFAs is therefore $2\log_2s+O(1)$, and the largest finite frozen-source index is asymptotic to $s^2$, by \eqref{ccc:dc:eq:clocks}. These are extremal asymptotics; the exact rank or least stabilization index of every individual automaton is not determined here.


\section{A sharp leading constant for every complete DFA}\label{ccc:dc:sec:leading-one}

The earlier general NFA bound $2(s-1)^2+3$ in the companion note (Part~V, Corollary~\ref{ccc:rs:cor:upper}) charges both boundaries by a global transient. For a DFA, a safe SCC permits a better allocation: the states used before entering that component and after leaving it are disjoint. A separate pumping observation makes the actual target letters compatible with stable alphabets after only $s$ positions at either boundary.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform deterministic rank bound]\label{ccc:dc:thm:leading-one}
Let $L$ be recognized by a complete $s$-state DFA over a finite nonempty alphabet. If $R(L)<\infty$, then
\[
R(L)\le s^2+3.
\]
The proof uses determinism and does not establish this bound for general NFAs.
\end{theorem}
\waddsevthree{For NFAs Part~VII proves $R(L)\le s^2+8s$ over two letters and
$R(L)\le s^2+\max\{8,2q-4\}s$ over $q$ letters
(Theorems~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:general} and~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:alphabet}), which also
gives $F_{\rm DFA}(s)\le s^2+8s$ for binary DFAs. The point of the theorem
here is the additive constant~$3$ for complete DFAs over every finite
alphabet, together with the deterministic lower family.}

For this proof choose a global transient $t\le(s-1)^2+1$ and a period for $E$ from Schwarz's theorem \cite[Theorem~4.3]{Schwarz}. Enlarge the period $p$ to a multiple of every integer $1,\ldots,s$; this does not change $t$. The resulting period need not be the particular $p$ used for the complexity certificate. Stable sets, live residues, and stripes are defined as before. The source's interior criterion and the stripe results above apply to any such common transient and period.

\begin{lemma}[Actual alphabets enter stable alphabets early]\label{ccc:dc:lem:early}
If $i\ge s$, then $P_i\subseteq P^\infty_{i\bmod p}$. If $j\ge s$, then $R_j\subseteq R^\infty_{j\bmod p}$. Consequently, for an actual hull word of total length $n$, its letter at position $i$ belongs to $C_{n\bmod p,i\bmod p}$ whenever
\[
i\ge s\qquad\text{and}\qquad n-i-1\ge s.
\]
The claims are inclusions, not equalities.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A walk of at least $s$ edges visits a vertex twice and contains a simple directed cycle of length $\ell\le s$. Since $\ell\mid p$, inserting $p/\ell$ extra copies of this cycle adds $p$ edges without changing endpoints. Repeating reaches arbitrarily large lengths in the same phase, proving the forward inclusion. Apply the same argument in the reversed graph for the backward inclusion.

For the letter statement, take its accepting seed witness with transition $q\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^c}q'$ at position $i$. Pump the witness prefix and suffix separately. The prefix proves stable forward membership of $q$, and appending $c$ proves stable forward membership of $q'$. The suffix proves stable backward membership of $q'$, and prepending $c$ proves stable backward membership of $q$. Thus both endpoints lie in the correct stable viable layers and $c$ is permitted there.
\end{proof}
\waddsevthree{The proof uses no determinism. Part~VII states the lemma for
NFAs, with a liveness clause, as Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:guards} and proves it
by this argument, which is printed only here.}

Fix a live residue $r$. Finite rank and the inherited safe-core criterion give a nonempty safe core. Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:stripe} supplies an entire viable stripe over an original cyclic SCC $C$, closed under every stable-permitted letter at its phase. Write $c=|C|$, $d=d_C$, and $\kappa$ for its cyclic-class function. Full-stripe membership depends only on the class/time offset modulo $d$, even though the phase graph is indexed modulo the possibly much larger $p$.

\begin{lemma}[A joint entry and exit budget]\label{ccc:dc:lem:budget}
For that stripe there exist an entry vertex $u\in C$, an exit vertex $v\in C$, an actual initial-to-$u$ path of length $h$, and an actual $v$-to-final path of length $\beta$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:dc:eq:budget}
h+\beta\le(s-c)d.
\end{equation}
The pair $(h\bmod p,u)$ belongs to the stripe, and for every $n\equiv r\pmod p$,
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:dc:eq:compatibility}
n-h-\beta\equiv\kappa(v)-\kappa(u)\pmod d.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Stable viability supplies an actual accepting walk through a stripe vertex at its prescribed phase, of total length $N\equiv r\pmod p$: choose a sufficiently long prefix and suffix from the stable reachability sets. Let $u$ be its first vertex in $C$ and $v$ its last. Between them the walk cannot leave $C$ and return, since any such outside vertex would be mutually reachable with $C$ and hence belong to it. Let the original entry time and exit length be $h_0$ and $\beta_0$.

In the prefix before entry, delete path segments between repeated pairs consisting of an outside state and elapsed time modulo $d$. Each deletion preserves the endpoint and length modulo $d$. The resulting path of length $h\equiv h_0\pmod d$ has distinct outside product vertices at all positions before entry. Shorten the suffix analogously to length $\beta\equiv\beta_0\pmod d$, with distinct outside product vertices at all positions after exit.

The outside states occurring before entry and after exit in the original walk are disjoint. A state occurring in both could reach $C$ and be reached from $C$, contradicting its being outside that SCC. The shortened paths use only these original states. If their outside-state counts are $a$ and $b$, then $h\le ad$, $\beta\le bd$, and $a+b\le s-c$, proving \eqref{ccc:dc:eq:budget}. This also covers zero-length access or exit paths.

The interior segment of the original walk has length congruent to $\kappa(v)-\kappa(u)$ modulo $d$. Because $d\mid p$, the two shortenings yield \eqref{ccc:dc:eq:compatibility}. The original entry pair lies in the stripe by the cyclic-class invariant along the path to the known stripe vertex. Preserving entry time modulo $d$ therefore keeps $(h\bmod p,u)$ in the same \emph{full} stripe, despite a possible change of its phase modulo $p$.
\end{proof}
\waddsevthree{The proof uses no determinism, only that the stripe is a full
stripe over an original SCC. Part~VII states the lemma for the universal
stripe of an NFA as Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:outside} and proves it by this
argument, which is printed only here.}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:leading-one}]
First assume $s\ge2$. For the component $C$, Schwarz's theorem supplies an internal relation-power transient
\[
1\le k\le(c-1)^2+1.
\]
For every length $\ell\ge k$, an internal walk from $x$ to $y$ exists exactly when $\ell\equiv\kappa(y)-\kappa(x)\pmod d$. To justify using the numerical threshold rather than an unspecified asymptotic one, enlarge its matrix period to a multiple of $d$ and propagate the equality of matrix entries forward from $\ell$ to the sufficiently long regime of the cyclic-class path criterion. The stable value is already attained at $\ell$.

Consider a nonempty hull word $w$ of length $n\ge t$, so its residue $r$ is live. Use the stripe and entry/exit paths above and set
\[
A=\max(h,s),\qquad B=\max(\beta+k,s).
\]
If $n\le A+B$, singleton fragments already give rank at most $A+B$. Otherwise, pad the $h$-step access path by an arbitrary internal $C$-walk of length $A-h$. Such a walk exists for every nonnegative length because each vertex of the cyclic strongly connected graph has an internal outgoing edge. The padded path reaches a stripe vertex at phase $A$.

Read the target central interval $w[A:n-B]$ from that vertex. Every letter is stable-permitted by Lemma~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:early}: its prefix length is at least $A\ge s$, and even its last letter has suffix length at least $B\ge s$. Stripe safety keeps the deterministic run inside $C$. Let $z$ be its endpoint. Its class is
\[
\kappa(z)\equiv\kappa(u)+(A-h)+(n-A-B)
=\kappa(u)+n-h-B\pmod d.
\]
Using \eqref{ccc:dc:eq:compatibility} gives
\[
\kappa(v)-\kappa(z)\equiv B-\beta\pmod d.
\]
Because $B-\beta\ge k$, there is an internal completion of exactly $B-\beta$ steps to $v$. Append the fixed $\beta$-step exit path. The resulting accepting seed has total length exactly $n$ and agrees with the target on its entire central interval. Hence that interval is one aligned admissible fragment, and
\begin{align*}
\rank_L(w)&\le A+B+1\\
&\le h+\beta+k+2s+1\\
&\le(s-c)d+(c-1)^2+2s+2\\
&\le(s-c)c+(c-1)^2+2s+2\\
&=c(s-2)+2s+3\le s^2+3.
\end{align*}
For $n<t$, the singleton bound is at most $n<t\le(s-1)^2+1$. Empty slices require no words to be bounded, and the empty-word convention contributes at most one. Finally a complete one-state DFA accepts either all words or none, so $R(L)=1$ and the asserted estimate also holds for $s=1$.
\end{proof}

Combining Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:leading-one} with the deterministic lower family gives
\[
\frac{(s-7)^2+2}{s^2}\le\frac{F_{\rm DFA}(s)}{s^2}\le1+\frac3{s^2}
\quad(s\ge8),
\]
and therefore the sharp leading constant one. The result does not determine the optimal lower-order terms or exact extremal function. Determinism enters through the safe-core characterization and its strict survivor-count decrease; nondeterministic images can expand, so this proof does not supply a leading-one NFA bound.
\waddsevthree{Part~VII supplies one by another route, robust compression in a
primitive component (Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:primitive}) in place of the
safe-core step; it uses Lemmas~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:early}
and~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:budget} in their NFA forms.}

\section{Finite verification and attribution}\label{ccc:dc:sec:checks}

The package contains four Python standard-library checkers and their saved outputs. They have different scopes.
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{check\_stripes\_exhaustive.py} finds the exact eventual cycle of $(P_i,R_i)$, enlarges its period to include SCC periods, computes the safe core by repeated deletion, and compares it with the stripe criterion. It checks every labelled complete binary DFA with fixed initial state and every final set on one, two, and three states: $2+64+5{,}832=5{,}898$ automata and $26{,}881$ live residues. It also checks uniform stripe viability.
\item \texttt{check\_stripes\_random.py} checks $1{,}519$ seeded random complete binary DFAs on one to eight states and all their live residues. Its random seed is $153735$.
\item \texttt{check\_rank\_family.py} verifies $24$ path-based alternating rank witnesses for $2\le m\le25$, reaching rank $578$. It also literally tests $14{,}308$ seed words for $2\le m\le8$ and $2\le n\le10$; these are seed-membership tests, not $14{,}308$ separate rank computations.
\item \texttt{check\_gate\_completion.py} independently verifies $1{,}829$ gate-state completion cases over $2\le m\le60$, the missing return at $g$, and $19$ alternating rank witnesses for $2\le m\le20$.
\end{itemize}
Run the scripts with assertions enabled, without Python's \texttt{-O} option. The tests corroborate the finite constructions and predicates. They do not prove the universal stripe lemmas, the complexity classification, correctness of every CRT reduction instance, or the quadratic asymptotic theorem. The proofs supply those arguments. The new uniform $s^2+3$ bound is an analytic deduction from the joint path budget; no finite test here exhausts that universal claim. No Lean or other proof-assistant formalization is included.

The contribution claimed relative to the pinned source is the SCC-stripe certificate and its $\coNP$ consequence, the crossover wrapper around classical modular testers, the complete-DFA quadratic-rank construction, and the joint entry/exit argument giving its sharp leading constant. The rank clocks, periodic interiors, deterministic safe cores, CRT arithmetic, selector-to-cycle devices, and relation-power bound are credited background. A comprehensive search excluding all equivalent earlier crossover results has not been completed, and no global first-discovery claim is made.

Open refinements include the following.
\wsevthree\ The manuscript lists them in one sentence; they are printed as
numbered questions, in the same order and words; their bracketed titles were
added.

\begin{question}[Exact DFA extremum]\label{ccc:dc:q:exact}
What is the exact $F_{\rm DFA}(s)$?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[Lower-order terms]\label{ccc:dc:q:lower-order}
What are the optimal lower-order terms beyond $s^2$?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[The deterministic family]\label{ccc:dc:q:family}
What is the exact rank of the displayed deterministic family?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[One automaton]\label{ccc:dc:q:individual}
What is the complexity of computing an individual DFA's exact finite rank or
least stabilization generation?
\end{question}

The sharp leading DFA rank constant and the finite-stabilization decision classification are settled by the results above, rather than left on that list.
\wsevthree\ The window of Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:rank} has width
$s^2+3-((s-7)^2+2)=14s-48$; Question~\ref{ccc:dc:q:exact} refines Part~IV's
Question~\ref{ccc:qr:q:constant} for complete DFAs, and
Question~\ref{ccc:dc:q:family} asks where in $[g+2,g+4]$ the rank of
$\Ldfa m$ lies. No statement of this Part is formalized; Part~II's
Question~\ref{ccc:ps:q:formal} applies to it as well.

\toprule
\textbf{Shipped path} & \textbf{Delivered as}\\
\midrule

\phantomsection\part*{Part VII.\quad Sharp leading constant for constrained crossover rank}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Part VII.\texorpdfstring{\quad}{ } Sharp leading constant for constrained crossover rank}

\noindent\emph{\wsevthree\ Part~VII was added on 1 October 2026 in batch~73,
from one manuscript written against Parts~I--III as printed above; it knew
Parts~IV and~V only as separate manuscripts and Part~VI not at all. It
answers Part~IV's Question~\ref{ccc:qr:q:constant} as to the leading
constant: for binary NFAs $(s-3)^2\le F(s)\le s^2+8s$, so
$F(s)/s^2\to1$, and for a $q$-letter alphabet the upper bound is
$s^2+\max\{8,2q-4\}s$. It computes $F(1),\ldots,F(4)=1,2,5,7$, the last
three with computer assistance, and gives an exact algorithm and a witness
cutoff for the maximal rank of one automaton. The exact $F(s)$ for $s\ge5$
stays open. Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:provenance} records the provenance and
fixes the notation of the addition.}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\wsevthree\ }{}Provenance and notation of Part~VII}

\subsection{The manuscript's summary and status}\label{ccc:sc:sec:abstract}

\emph{Abstract, as delivered.} For every $s$-state $\eps$-free NFA over a
$q$-letter alphabet, $q\ge2$, with finite maximal aligned-fragment rank, we
prove $R(L)\le s^2+\max\{8,2q-4\}s$. For every $s\ge4$, an explicit family
using every alphabet symbol has $R(L)=(s-3)^2$. Hence $F_q(s)/s^2\to1$ for
each fixed alphabet and even when $q=o(s)$. The proof combines
primitive-graph compression, a circulation-based common-anchor theorem for
selected monochromatic cycles, and a reduction through periodic viable
layers. We also give an exact weighted-parser algorithm and a complete
witness-length cutoff. Independent exhaustive computations establish the
binary values $F_2(1)=1$, $F_2(2)=2$, $F_2(3)=5$, and $F_2(4)=7$, with a
proved transient pruning rule in the four-state case. For every $s\ge3$, a
uniform $s$-state family has slice maxima $\min(N,2s-1)$ for $N\ge1$ and
attains the exponent bound $R=2e+1$ with $e=s-1$. Sharper Hamiltonian bounds
and a quadratic-exponent, linear-rank barrier family are retained. Ordinary
all-size proofs are distinguished from the computer-assisted censuses and
finite consistency checks. No exact formula for $F_2(s)$ at $s\ge5$,
unrestricted alphabet-growth leading coefficient, proof-assistant
certification, or literature-wide priority is claimed.

\emph{Status, as delivered.} The manuscript has no separate status box; its
abstract ends with the disclaimer above, and
Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:scope} states its scope and the three roles of its
evidence. Its delivery README adds: no alphabet-size-independent additive
term or leading-one conclusion for unrestricted alphabet growth is claimed;
the article keeps $R(\varnothing)=1$, whereas the census programs record an
empty hull as raw value $0$, a difference that affects no extremal value;
the four-state counts are pruned representative counts, not a full
four-state rank distribution; the bounded tests do not substitute for an
all-size proof; this is an unrefereed note with ordinary and
computer-assisted proofs, not a proof-assistant certificate; no
literature-wide priority claim is made. It also says that every page of the
delivered PDF was visually inspected, and that raw million-row listings and
compiled executables were omitted from the package.

\section{Aligned fragments and the main results}\label{ccc:sc:sec:intro}

\wsevthree\ ``The preceding reports'' and ``the pinned report'' of this Part
are this report as it stood at the pin, Parts~I--III; ``the companion
notes'' are the manuscripts of Parts~IV and~V. The manuscript's citations of
all three are replaced by internal references. Its $F_2(s)$ is Part~II's
$F(s)$, and its $R_j$ (written $B_j$ in the manuscript) are Part~II's
backward layers; Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:notation} lists the other symbols.

Let $\Sigma$ be a nonempty finite alphabet. The definitions below apply to every such alphabet; Sections~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:primitive}--\ref{ccc:sc:sec:transfer} first establish the binary upper bound, with $\Sigma=\{0,1\}$. For $L\subseteq\Sigma^*$ write $L_N=L\cap\Sigma^N$. Boundaries are indexed from zero: $w[u:v]$ consists of the letters in positions $u+1,\ldots,v$.

\begin{definition}
If $L_N\ne\varnothing$ and $N\ge1$, the \emph{coordinate hull} at length $N$ is
\[
 \rec(L)_N=\prod_{i=1}^N\{x_i:x\in L_N\}.
\]
It is empty when $L_N$ is empty, and $\rec(L)_0=L_0$. Put $\rec(L)=\bigcup_{N\ge0}\rec(L)_N$.
For a target $w\in\Sigma^N$, a nonempty interval $[u,v)$ is \emph{admissible} if a full seed $x\in L_N$ satisfies $x[u:v]=w[u:v]$. Its \emph{aligned-fragment rank} $\rank_L(w)$ is the least number of consecutive admissible intervals partitioning $[0,N)$, or $\infty$ if none exist. Set $\rank_L(\eps)=1$ if $\eps\in L$, and $\infty$ otherwise. Finally define
\[
 R(L)=\sup\bigl(\{1\}\cup\{\rank_L(w):w\in\rec(L)\}\bigr).
\]
\end{definition}

Every fragment has its original position and a seed of the same full length. Different fragments may use different seeds. In particular, finite rank is equivalent to hull membership, and $\rank_L(w)\le N$ for every nonempty hull word of length $N$. The supremum defining $R(L)$ excludes words outside the hull; its value can still be infinite if the finite ranks are unbounded. These are the fragment conventions used for constrained crossover in the preceding reports (Part~I's Definitions~\ref{ccc:def:hull} and~\ref{ccc:def:rank}, Part~II's Definition~\ref{ccc:ps:def:rank} and Corollary~\ref{ccc:ps:cor:bounded}, and Part~V's Section~\ref{ccc:rs:sec:setting}).

An $\eps$-free NFA here consists of a state set $Q$, transition relations $\Delta_a\subseteq Q\times Q$ for $a\in\Sigma$, and sets $I,F\subseteq Q$. Its underlying digraph has edge relation $E=\bigcup_{a\in\Sigma}\Delta_a$. An edge may carry several letters. For $S\subseteq Q$ and $x\in\Sigma^*$, let $\delta(S,x)$ be the possible endpoints of paths labeled $x$ from $S$.
The NFA is \emph{factor-universal} if
\[
 \delta(Q,x)\ne\varnothing\qquad\text{for every }x\in\Sigma^*.
\]
Thus all words label some walk, with both endpoints unrestricted. This is a condition on the labeled graph, not a claim that $L=\Sigma^*$ for the chosen $I,F$.

A digraph is \emph{primitive} if some positive length admits a walk between every ordered pair of vertices; its least such length is its \emph{exponent} $e$. Equivalently, it is strongly connected and its cycle lengths have gcd one. A Hamilton cycle visits all vertices once.

\begin{theorem}[General binary NFA bound]\label{ccc:sc:thm:general}
Let an $s$-state, $\varepsilon$-free binary NFA have arbitrary initial and final sets, where $s\ge1$. If $R(L)<\infty$, then
\[
 \boxed{R(L)\le s^2+8s.}
\]
Consequently, if $F_2(s)$ is the supremum of finite values $R(L)$ over binary NFAs with at most $s$ states, then
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:sharp}
 (s-3)^2\le F_2(s)\le s^2+8s\quad(s\ge4),
 \qquad \boxed{\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{F_2(s)}{s^2}=1.}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

For alphabet size $q\ge2$, write $F_q(s)$ for the supremum of finite ranks recognized by NFAs over a fixed $q$-letter alphabet with at most $s$ states. The subscript always records alphabet size and the argument records state count. The same extremal convention results from using exactly $s$ states, since unreachable states may be added. The main generalization, proved in Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:alphabet}, is
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:main-alphabets}
 \boxed{(s-3)^2\le F_q(s)\le s^2+\max\{8,2q-4\}s\quad(q\ge2,\ s\ge4).}
\end{equation}
Thus the leading coefficient is one for each fixed finite alphabet and for every alphabet-size sequence $q=o(s)$. In particular $s^2+8s$ works for every alphabet of size two through six. The exact binary small-state results, established separately in Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:census}, are $1,2,5,7$ for one through four states. The subscript in $F_q(s)$ must not be confused with the state argument: the ternary function is $F_3(s)$, while the binary three-state value is $F_2(3)=5$.

Sections~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:primitive}--\ref{ccc:sc:sec:transfer} first prove the binary upper bound, using two monochromatic cycles and an alphabet-independent phase-component reduction. Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:lower} proves the quadratic lower construction. Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:alphabet} supplies the small-family common-anchor theorem and extends the bound to every finite alphabet. Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:linear} gives the sharp linear family and its exact slice profile. Sections~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:exact}--\ref{ccc:sc:sec:census} separate the exact algorithm and pruning proofs from the exhaustive binary computations. Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:restricted} preserves sharper bounds for Hamiltonian and near-Hamiltonian graphs, followed by the barrier to a total-state-subset approach. None of the arguments assumes determinism, a single initial state, or a total labeled state subset.

The leading-coefficient result sharpens the earlier binary comparison $(s-3)^2\le F_2(s)\le2(s-1)^2+3$ from the companion notes (Part~IV's Theorem~\ref{ccc:qr:thm:main} and Part~V's Corollary~\ref{ccc:rs:cor:upper}). The proof here is complete apart from the explicitly cited classical primitive-digraph exponent results. The periodic-interior implication needed for the reduction is reproved, and no global transient estimate enters the leading-coefficient bound.

\wsevthree\ Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:general} answers Part~IV's
Question~\ref{ccc:qr:q:constant} as to the leading constant, which is one,
for NFAs over any fixed finite alphabet. Two consequences are the write's
observations, not the manuscript's. First, the bound $s^2+8s$ needs no
assumption on the initial states, so with Part~IV's single-initial-state
family (Section~\ref{ccc:qr:sec:upper}) it gives
$(s-4)^2\le F_{\rm one}(s)\le s^2+8s$ and $F_{\rm one}(s)/s^2\to1$ as well.
Second, it does not make Part~V's bound obsolete for small $s$:
$2(s-1)^2+3<s^2+8s$ exactly when $s\le11$ (for example $203<209$ at
$s=11$, and $240<245$ the other way at $s=12$). For complete DFAs,
Part~VI's $s^2+3$ (Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:leading-one}) is smaller than
both.

\section{Robust compression inside a primitive component}\label{ccc:sc:sec:primitive}

The general reduction needs more than a rank theorem for a component's own coordinate hull. An interval taken from a hull word of the whole NFA need not belong to that smaller hull. The following formulation explicitly permits its singleton witnesses to come from outside the component.

\begin{definition}
Fix vertices $u,v$ in a labeled graph and a target $x$ of length $M\ge1$. A \emph{permissive partition} is a consecutive partition of $x$ in which each piece is either a singleton with no internal witness required, or agrees on the same interval with the label of a complete $M$-edge walk from $u$ to $v$. The endpoints and full length of these witnesses are fixed; different pieces may use different walks.
\end{definition}

\begin{samepage}
\begin{theorem}[Robust primitive bound]\label{ccc:sc:thm:primitive}
Let an $n$-vertex binary labeled graph be primitive and factor-universal. For every $u,v$, every $M\ge1$, and every binary target of length $M$, there is a permissive partition with at most
\[
 n^2+8n
\]
pieces. Consequently any NFA with this graph and nonempty initial and final sets satisfies $R(L)\le n^2+8n$.
\end{theorem}
\end{samepage}

The consequence follows by choosing $u\in I$, $v\in F$: replace every licensed singleton by its full-language hull witness. The assertion does not claim that every short length admits an internal $u$-to-$v$ walk. At such lengths an all-singleton partition is allowed.

\subsection{Exponent reduction and the two-cycle geometry}

For a primitive graph of exponent $e$, a target of length $M>2e$ has a single internally witnessed middle interval $[e,M-e)$. Indeed, factor universality gives a path labeled by that interval, and $e$-step walks from $u$ and to $v$ complete it to length $M$. The two boundaries can be licensed singletons. Hence the permissive rank is at most $2e+1$; when $M\le2e$, use only singletons.

Put $\omega_n=(n-1)^2+1$. The classical results of Kirkland, Olesky and van den Driessche \cite[Theorems~1.1--1.2 and the proof of Theorem~1.2]{KOV} give the following input. If $n\ge6$ and
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:general-threshold}
 e\ge\lfloor\omega_n/2\rfloor+2,
\end{equation}
then the only elementary cycle lengths are two coprime integers $j<k\le n$, and
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:general-exponent}
 e\le n+j(k-2).
\end{equation}
Below this threshold, $2e+1\le(n-1)^2+4$. If $j\le n/2$, equation~\eqref{ccc:sc:eq:general-exponent} gives $e\le n^2/2$. For $2\le n\le5$, the classical bound $e\le\omega_n$ suffices, and the one-state graph has both loop labels. All these cases satisfy Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:primitive}. It remains to treat
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:longcycles}
 n\ge6,\qquad n/2<j<k\le n,\qquad \gcd(j,k)=1,\qquad e\le jk.
\end{equation}
The final inequality follows from $n-2j<0$ in \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:general-exponent}.

\begin{lemma}[Order of cycle intersections]\label{ccc:sc:lem:order}
If a digraph has only the elementary cycle lengths $j<k$, where $j\ge3$ and $\gcd(j,k)=1$, any two elementary cycles encounter their common vertices in the same cyclic order.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Otherwise there are common vertices $a,b,c$ ordered $a,b,c$ on one cycle and $a,c,b$ on the other. The path from $a$ to $b$ on the first cycle followed by the path from $b$ to $a$ on the second is a nonempty closed walk. Do the same for $b$ to $c$ and for $c$ to $a$. These three nonempty closed walks partition the two original cycles' edge multiset, with multiplicity. Decomposing them into elementary cycles produces at least three cycles whose total length is $2j$, $j+k$, or $2k$.

Each such total has a unique nonnegative representation $Aj+Bk$, namely its original representation $A_0j+B_0k$ with $A_0+B_0=2$. To see this, any other representation has $B=B_0+t j$ and $A=A_0-t k$ for an integer $t$. Since $A_0,B_0\le2<j<k$, neither sign of nonzero $t$ preserves nonnegativity. Every decomposition must therefore contain exactly two elementary cycles, a contradiction. With fewer than three common vertices the order assertion is automatic.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A circular chain of alternatives]\label{ccc:sc:lem:bubbles}
Suppose two elementary cycles meet and satisfy Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:order}. Their union is a circular chain of pairs of directed paths between consecutive common vertices, allowing internally simple return paths to the same vertex when there is just one common vertex. Each pair either is a single shared edge or consists of two internally disjoint paths, called a \emph{bubble}. Choices of paths in different bubbles are independent and produce elementary cycles. If the two original cycles have equal length, every bubble has equal-length alternatives. If their lengths are $j<k$, exactly one bubble has unequal alternatives, of lengths differing by $k-j$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Between consecutive common vertices, the two paths have no further common internal vertex; distinct pairs have disjoint interiors. Thus any choice visits each common vertex once and is an elementary cycle. If both original cycles have length $j$, replacing any one path cannot shorten a cycle below $j$, so every difference, measured from the first cycle to the second, is nonnegative. Their sum is zero, so each difference is zero. For two $k$-cycles use the analogous prohibition against exceeding $k$.

For cycles of lengths $j<k$, every difference measured from the $j$-cycle is nonnegative. A positive difference must be exactly $k-j$, because otherwise a single replacement would produce a cycle of a forbidden intermediate length. The differences sum to $k-j$, proving uniqueness of the unequal bubble. The one-common-vertex case consists of a single bubble and obeys the same argument.
\end{proof}

Factor universality supplies a monochromatic elementary cycle $C_0$ for $0$ and one $C_1$ for $1$: read a constant word of length at least $n$ and extract a cycle. Under \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:longcycles}, these cycles meet, since each has more than $n/2$ vertices. Write $U=C_0\cup C_1$ and $m=|V(U)|\le n$. Every shared edge carries both labels.

\begin{lemma}[Equal-bubble fragments]\label{ccc:sc:lem:bubble-fragments}
Inside a complete walk that traverses the bubbles of $U$, a length-$t$ equal bubble requires cuts only where consecutive target letters differ within that bubble. No cut is required just because the target crosses a common vertex or a shared edge. Give charge zero to shared edges and to the first edge of each equal bubble path, and charge one to its other edges. A segment meeting only equal-length bubbles and shared edges, of charge $W$ and with every touched bubble in a complete ambient traversal, can be covered by at most $W+1$ internally witnessed fragments.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
After cutting at each internal symbol change, every fragment's portion in a given bubble is constant. Choose its $0$- or $1$-monochromatic branch accordingly. Choices for different bubbles are independent, and each preserves both endpoints and length. Shared edges permit both symbols. Thus a fragment can cross a bubble endpoint and continue into further bubbles without another cut.

For a fragment starting or ending inside a bubble, replace the whole containing traversal in its complete witness walk, including the portion outside the fragment. The witness still has the original total length and endpoints. Each possible internal change is charged to the second of its two edges, so the number of cuts is at most $W$. Different fragments may use different branch choices.
\end{proof}

All applications below use a repeated-cycle block based at a common vertex of $C_0,C_1$. Its bubbles are therefore complete eligible traversals, even when the segment being covered cuts through one of them.

\subsection{Equal lengths of the monochromatic cycles}

Suppose $|C_0|=|C_1|=\ell\in\{j,k\}$ and write $r$ for the other length. Set $h=m-\ell$. If the equal bubbles have lengths $t_1,\ldots,t_b$, vertex counting gives
\[
 h=\sum_{i=1}^b(t_i-1).
\]
Every elementary cycle of $U$ has length $\ell$ and charge $h$ under Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:bubble-fragments}. Put $d_0=h/\ell$, so $0\le d_0<1$, since $m\le n<2\ell$. Successively deleting elementary cycles from a $U$-walk segment of length $x$ leaves a simple path of at most $m-1$ edges, each of charge at most one. Its total charge is consequently at most
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:equal-charge}
 d_0x+(1-d_0)(m-1).
\end{equation}
Cycle deletion only counts charge; fragments stay at the original positions.

Choose an elementary $r$-cycle $J$. It meets $C_0$ because $r+\ell>n$. Let $z\in J\cap C_0$ and let $y\in C_0\cap C_1$. Choose a path from $u$ to $z$ of length $a\le n-1$, the forward $C_0$ path from $z$ to $y$ of length $t\le\ell-1$, and a path from $y$ to $v$ of length $b\le n-1$. Their combined length is
\[
 D_0=a+t+b\le2n+\ell-3.
\]
For $M-D_0\ge(\ell-1)(r-1)$, the elementary two-generator Frobenius formula yields
\[
 M-D_0=\alpha r+\beta\ell,\qquad \alpha,\beta\ge0.
\]
Reducing $\alpha$ modulo $\ell$ increases $\beta$ and gives $0\le\alpha<\ell$. Use one complete $M$-edge walk, in this order:
\[
 u\to z;\quad J^{\alpha};\quad z\to y\text{ along }C_0;
 \quad C_0^{\beta}\text{ at }y;\quad y\to v.
\]
Its single $J$ block has length $K_0=\alpha r<jk$. Replacements inside the repeated-$C_0$ block are legitimate complete-walk witnesses.

If the representation threshold fails, the singleton bound is enough, because
\[
 M<D_0+(\ell-1)(r-1)\le jk+2n-r-2<n^2+2n.
\]
Otherwise cover the whole target if $M\le2e$, or only the two windows $[0,e)$ and $[M-e,M)$ if $M>2e$, supplementing them with the universal middle fragment. Let $T\le2e$ be the number of covered window positions and $K$ the number lying in the single $J$ block. Importantly, $K\le K_0<jk$ \emph{in total across both windows}. The fixed connecting paths cost at most $D_0$ licensed singletons. The repeated-$C_0$ portions form at most two segments. Equations~\eqref{ccc:sc:eq:equal-charge} and \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:longcycles} give a total of at most
\begin{align*}
 d_0T+(1-d_0)K+D_0+2(1-d_0)(m-1)+3
 &\le 2d_0 e+(1-d_0)jk+D_0+2m+1\\
 &\le jk(1+d_0)+D_0+2m+1\\
 &=rm+D_0+2m+1\\
 &\le rn+4n+\ell-2\le n^2+5n.
\end{align*}
The constant three allows one extra piece for each of the at most two repeated-cycle segments and one middle piece. If the monochromatic cycles coincide, $h=0$ and every edge is doubly labeled; the argument remains valid.

\subsection{Unequal lengths of the monochromatic cycles}

After interchanging symbols if necessary, take $|C_0|=j$ and $|C_1|=k$. Let the unique unequal bubble have path lengths $p_0$ and $q_0=p_0+k-j$. If $B$ is the sum of $t-1$ over the equal bubbles, then
\[
 m=j+B+q_0-1.
\]
Use the equal-bubble charges already defined, but charge every edge of the unequal bubble by one, with an additional unit on its first edge. Thus edge charges belong to $\{0,1,2\}$. A length-$a$ traversal of the unequal bubble has charge $a+1$, enough to cut before it and after every position and to use licensed singleton pieces there. Cuts outside the covered segment are unnecessary. In the other bubbles, Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:bubble-fragments} applies. Any segment has a permissive partition of size at most its charge plus one, also when it starts or ends inside the unequal bubble.

The charges of the $j$- and $k$-cycles are respectively
\[
 B+p_0+1=m-k+2,\qquad B+q_0+1=m-j+2.
\]
Since $j+k\ge n+2\ge m+2$, their maximum mean charge is
\[
 \mu=\frac{m-j+2}{k}\le1,
\]
for
\[
 j(m-j+2)-k(m-k+2)=(k-j)(j+k-m-2)\ge0.
\]
All cycles in $U$ arise by branch choices, so these are the only possible means. Deleting cycles leaves a simple path of at most $m-1$ edges, each of charge at most two. A segment of length $x$ therefore has charge at most
\[
 \mu x+(2-\mu)(m-1).
\]

Choose a common vertex $y$ and fixed paths $u\to y$ and $y\to v$ with total length $D_0\le2n-2$. If $M-D_0\ge(j-1)(k-1)$, write $M-D_0=\alpha j+\beta k$ and use the complete walk consisting of the entry path, $\alpha$ copies of $C_0$ and $\beta$ copies of $C_1$ based at $y$, and the exit path. Below this threshold the singleton bound is less than $n^2+2n$.

As before, cover the full target when $M\le2e$, or the two boundary windows otherwise, with a separate middle fragment. There are at most two covered $U$ segments. The number of pieces is at most
\begin{align*}
 2\mu e+D_0+2(2-\mu)(m-1)+3
 &\le2j(m-j+2)+6n-3\\
 &\le2j(n+2-j)+6n-3\\
 &\le\frac{(n+2)^2}{2}+6n-3\\
 &=\frac{n^2}{2}+8n-1\le n^2+8n.
\end{align*}
Together with the preliminary cases, this proves Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:primitive}. In particular, every nonsingleton witness has exactly the required $M$ edges from the fixed $u$ to the fixed $v$. No target-hull assumption for the component entered the proof.
\section{Stable phases and a universal cyclic component}\label{ccc:sc:sec:periodic}

We supply the finite-rank implication of the periodic-interior criterion from Part~II (Theorem~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:criterion}). Only an eventual period is needed here; its size will never be charged to rank.

For the underlying relation $E$, use the exact-length layers
\[
 P_i=E^i(I),\qquad R_j=\{q:\text{a $j$-edge walk leads from $q$ to }F\}.
\]
For a target of length $N$, a word $x$ can occur as an aligned seed fragment on $[i,j)$ exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:fragment-test}
 \delta(P_i,x)\cap R_{N-j}\ne\varnothing.
\end{equation}
The forward implication follows by restricting an accepting walk; for the converse, append arbitrary-label prefix and suffix walks of lengths $i$ and $N-j$. Intermediate states lie in the viable layers $P_a\cap R_{N-a}$.

The pair $(P_i,R_i)$ evolves deterministically on a finite set, so it has a common eventual threshold $t$ and period $p$. Enlarge $p$ to be divisible by every integer from $1$ to $s$; this does not change the threshold. Denote the stable layers by $P_a^\infty,R_a^\infty$, for $a\in\mathbb Z/p\mathbb Z$. A total residue $r$ is live if $P_r^\infty\cap F\ne\varnothing$. For a live $r$, define
\begin{align*}
 S_{r,a}&=P_a^\infty\cap R_{r-a}^\infty,\\
 \Gamma_{r,a}&=\{b\in\{0,1\}:\Delta_b(S_{r,a})\cap S_{r,a+1}\ne\varnothing\}.
\end{align*}
At sufficiently interior coordinates of a sufficiently large accepted length in residue $r$, these are exactly the viable state sets and coordinate alphabets. They are nonempty at every phase: an arbitrarily long accepting walk in that residue passes through every phase in its interior.

\wsevthree\ These are Part~II's definitions~\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:PR},
\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:fragment-test}, \eqref{ccc:ps:eq:periodicity},
\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:live} and~\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:stable-states}; the stable
alphabet $\Gamma_{r,a}$ is Part~II's $C_{r,a}$~\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:stable-letters},
kept under the manuscript's name because $C$ denotes a component and $C_q$ a
constant below. The enlargement of $p$ is the one made in Part~VI,
Section~\ref{ccc:dc:sec:leading-one}.

Form a phase graph with vertices $(q,a)$ for $q\in S_{r,a}$, and edges $(q,a)\xrightarrow{b}(q',a+1)$ whenever $(q,q')\in\Delta_b$. A word is phase-admissible at $a$ when its successive symbols belong to $\Gamma_{r,a},\Gamma_{r,a+1},\ldots$.

\begin{lemma}[Finite rank forces all stable words to lift; Part~II, restated]\label{ccc:sc:lem:interior}
If $R(L)<\infty$, every phase-admissible word at every live residue and starting phase labels a path in the phase graph.
\end{lemma}
\noindent\wsevthree\ This is the implication (1)$\Rightarrow$(3) of Part~II's
Theorem~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:criterion}, proved there by
Theorem~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:pumping} for any $t,p$
satisfying~\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:periodicity}. The manuscript proves it again by
the same pumping argument (pad a missing word to a block of length divisible
by $p$, embed $k$ copies between an accepted prefix and suffix, and count one
internal cut per copy); that proof is not reprinted.

\begin{lemma}[A universal strongly connected component]\label{ccc:sc:lem:universal-scc}
In a finite phase graph with nonempty alphabets at every phase, if every phase-admissible word can be read, some strongly connected component reads every phase-admissible word at every starting phase.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If a component misses a positive-length word $v$ at phase $b$, then at any phase $a$ prepend arbitrary admissible symbols of length congruent to $b-a\pmod p$. The resulting word is missing in that component at phase $a$. Thus failure anywhere gives an obstruction starting at every phase; if a component lacks a phase, any permitted one-letter word there supplies a positive-length obstruction.

If no component is universal, starting at any current phase concatenate one appropriately aligned obstruction for each component, updating the phase after each word. The resulting block cannot be read wholly within any component. Repeat this construction $h$ times, where $h$ is the number of components. Every successful walk would cross a component boundary inside each block, contradicting the fact that a walk in the condensation acyclic graph can change components at most $h-1$ times. This contradicts universality. The universal component is cyclic, since it reads arbitrarily long words.
\end{proof}

For a strongly connected cyclic digraph $C$ of period $d$, fix cyclic classes $\kappa:C\to\mathbb Z/d\mathbb Z$, so every edge increases $\kappa$ by one. We use the standard cyclic-class path property: all sufficiently large lengths $a$ admit a walk $u\to v$ exactly when $a\equiv\kappa(v)-\kappa(u)\pmod d$. This follows, for example, by restricting the $d$th relation power to a cyclic class, where it is primitive.

\begin{lemma}[The whole cyclic stripe is present]\label{ccc:sc:lem:stripe}
Every cyclic strongly connected component of the viable phase graph has the form
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:stripe}
 K=\{(v,b):v\in C,\ b-a\equiv\kappa(v)-\kappa(q)\pmod d\}
\end{equation}
for an original strongly connected component $C$, a pair $(q,a)\in K$, and the period $d$ of $C$. Every internal edge between stripe pairs is present. If $K$ is the universal component of Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:universal-scc}, then $\Gamma_{r,b}$ is precisely the union of the labels on internal edges of $C$ leaving its stripe class at phase $b$. Consequently the stable alphabet sequence has period dividing $d$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Project paths and return paths in $K$ to identify the original component $C$. A cyclic phase component projects a nonempty closed walk, so $C$ is cyclic. Along any path the difference of phase and cyclic class stays fixed, proving one containment in \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:stripe}.

For a compatible pair $(v,b)$, choose sufficiently long internal walks $q\to v$ and $v\to q$ whose lengths are respectively $b-a$ and $a-b$ modulo $p$. Such lengths exist because $d\mid p$ and the prescribed residues have the correct cyclic-class differences. Concatenate the first walk to a stable prefix reaching $q$ at phase $a$, and concatenate the second before a stable suffix leaving $q$ with complementary phase $r-a$. This gives stable prefix and suffix access to $v$ at the required phases. Thus $(v,b)$ is viable. The same construction applies to all intermediate compatible pairs, so these internal walks belong to the phase graph and show mutual reachability. This proves equality.

Every internal edge advances the cyclic class, hence joins stripe pairs and is present in the viable graph. Its label belongs to the stable alphabet. Conversely, universality within $K$ reads every permitted one-letter word, so every stable symbol occurs on such an internal edge. The leaving cyclic class is determined by $b\bmod d$, giving the final periodicity statement.
\end{proof}

This argument extracts a universal \emph{component}, not a forward-total set of original states. No step uses deterministic images or a decrease in subset cardinality.

\wsevthree\ For a DFA, Part~VI reaches a full stripe from the other side: a
nonempty safe core contains a whole viable stripe with no permitted exit
(Lemmas~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:viability}--\ref{ccc:dc:lem:propagation} and
Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:stripe}). Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:stripe} is the NFA
counterpart for a universal component; the two share the core step (long
internal walks of the right residue, $d\mid p$) but state different
things.
\section{Transferring the component bound to the whole NFA}\label{ccc:sc:sec:transfer}

Two quantitative facts keep a possibly large phase period from entering the rank estimate. The first uses only linear guards; the second charges entry and exit to disjoint outside states.

\begin{lemma}[Actual slices and stable alphabets; Part~VI, for NFAs]\label{ccc:sc:lem:guards}
Every accepted length $N\ge s$ has a live residue modulo $p$. Moreover,
\[
 P_i\subseteq P_{i\bmod p}^\infty\quad(i\ge s),\qquad
 R_j\subseteq R_{j\bmod p}^\infty\quad(j\ge s).
\]
If $w\in\rec(L)_N$ and $s\le i<N-s$, its symbol at zero-based position $i$ belongs to $\Gamma_{N\bmod p,i\bmod p}$.
\end{lemma}
\noindent\wsevthree\ This is Part~VI's Lemma~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:early}, stated
there for complete DFAs, with one more clause (liveness of every accepted
length $N\ge s$). The manuscript's proof is Part~VI's, almost word for word:
a walk of at least $s$ edges contains an elementary cycle of length
$\ell\le s$, and inserting $p/\ell$ copies of it adds $p$ edges without moving
its endpoints; pumping the prefix and the suffix of a seed witness separately
puts the endpoints of the witnessed letter into the stable layers. That
proof uses no determinism, so it proves the lemma for NFAs as stated; applied
to a whole accepting walk of length $N\ge s$ it also gives the liveness
clause. It is not reprinted. The manuscript does not cite Part~VI, which was
written separately; only inclusion, not equality with the stable alphabets,
is asserted in both.

\begin{lemma}[Joint entry and exit budget; Part~VI, for NFAs]\label{ccc:sc:lem:outside}
Fix a live residue and its universal stripe over an original component $C$ of size $c$ and period $d$. There are fixed paths from an initial state to a vertex $u\in C$ and from a vertex $v\in C$ to a final state, of lengths $h,\beta$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:outside-budget}
 h+\beta\le(s-c)d.
\end{equation}
For every $N$ in the live residue,
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:outside-residue}
 N-h-\beta\equiv\kappa(v)-\kappa(u)\pmod d,
\end{equation}
and $(u,h\bmod p)$ belongs to that stripe.
\end{lemma}
\noindent\wsevthree\ This is Part~VI's Lemma~\ref{ccc:dc:lem:budget}, with
the universal stripe of Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:universal-scc} in place of
Part~VI's safe stripe; \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:outside-budget}
and~\eqref{ccc:sc:eq:outside-residue} are \eqref{ccc:dc:eq:budget}
and~\eqref{ccc:dc:eq:compatibility}. The manuscript's proof is Part~VI's, step
for step: take a long accepting walk through a stripe pair, let $u$ and $v$
be its first and last vertices in $C$, shorten the parts before $u$ and after
$v$ by deleting segments between repeated pairs (outside state, elapsed time
modulo $d$), and note that the outside states used before entry and after
exit are disjoint. That proof uses only that the stripe is a full stripe of
viable pairs over an original strongly connected component, not
determinism, so it proves the lemma as stated. It is not reprinted.

\subsection{An imprimitive component}

Suppose $d\ge2$. Choose a cyclic class of minimum size $m$, so $m\le c/d$. The internal $d$-step relation on this class is primitive. Its exponent $e_0$ obeys
\[
 e_0\le(m-1)^2+1
\]
by the classical primitive exponent bound; this conservative formula also holds at $m=1$.

For any $u',v'\in C$, follow internal edges from $u'$ for $r'<d$ steps to the chosen class, and follow internal predecessor edges backwards from $v'$ for $t'<d$ steps to that class. There is an internal outgoing and incoming edge at every vertex, and the cyclic classes fix these two residues. Every length $L$ of compatible residue with
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:imprimitive-threshold}
 L\ge T:=d\bigl((m-1)^2+1\bigr)+2d-2
       \le\frac{c^2}{d}+2d-2
\end{equation}
can join the two selected vertices: $L-r'-t'$ is a multiple of $d$ and is at least $de_0$. Thus every compatible pair has every compatible length at least $T$. The final estimate uses $(m-1)^2+1\le m^2$.

For a hull target of length $N\ge s$, use its live residue and the paths of Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:outside}. Put
\[
 A=\max(h+T,s),\qquad B=\max(\beta+T,s).
\]
If $N\le A+B$, use only singletons. Otherwise the target interval $w[A:N-B]$ follows the stable alphabet profile by Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:guards}. The universal stripe supplies an internal lift with the appropriate starting cyclic class. Equation~\eqref{ccc:sc:eq:imprimitive-threshold} connects $u$ to its first vertex in exactly $A-h$ steps and its last vertex to $v$ in exactly $B-\beta$ steps. The stripe congruence and \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:outside-residue} give the two required residues. Appending the fixed outside paths produces a full accepted seed of exactly $N$ edges matching this interval.

In both length cases the rank is at most
\begin{align}
 A+B+1
 &\le(s-c)d+\frac{2c^2}{d}+4d+2s-3.\label{ccc:sc:eq:imprimitive-rank}
\end{align}
For fixed $s,c$, the function $f(d)=(s-c)d+2c^2/d$ is convex on $[2,c]$, and
\[
 f(2)=c^2+2(s-c)\le s^2,\qquad
 f(c)=c(s-c+2)\le cs\le s^2.
\]
The second inequality uses $c\ge2$. The maximum of a convex function on an interval is attained at an endpoint, so \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:imprimitive-rank} gives
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:imprimitive-result}
 \rank_L(w)\le s^2+6s-3.
\end{equation}
Unequal cyclic-class sizes and phase-dependent singleton or binary alphabets are all included. We did not recode blocks as letters or apply a binary theorem to a larger block alphabet.

\subsection{A primitive component with both symbols}

Now $d=1$. Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:stripe} says that the stable alphabet is constant and equals the set of internal labels in $C$. If it is $\{0,1\}$, the component is primitive and factor-universal, and $h+\beta\le s-c$.

If $N<h+\beta$, singletons already suffice with at most $s$ pieces. Otherwise put $M=N-h-\beta$ and apply Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:primitive} to the \emph{actual} substring $w[h:N-\beta]$ and fixed endpoints $u,v$. At $M=0$ use the empty partition. Every internally witnessed piece extends by the fixed outside paths to a complete global seed of exactly $N$ edges. Every freely licensed singleton instead uses its witness from the global coordinate hull. The outside positions are also covered by global singleton witnesses. Thus
\begin{align*}
 \rank_L(w)&\le h+\beta+c^2+8c\\
 &\le s-c+c^2+8c\le s^2+8s,
\end{align*}
since $s^2+8s-(s-c+c^2+8c)=(s-c)(s+c+7)\ge0$.

No extra stable-alphabet guard is required in this case: every binary symbol is in the core alphabet. We do not infer isolated-core hull membership. The permissive form of Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:primitive} is exactly what makes short lengths and externally licensed singletons legitimate here.

\subsection{A primitive component with one symbol}

The remaining alphabet is $\{\gamma\}$ for one binary symbol $\gamma$. Every internal edge then carries $\gamma$ alone. Let $e_C\le(c-1)^2+1$ be the exponent of $C$ and $M=N-h-\beta$.

If $M<e_C$, singleton fragments give
\[
 \rank_L(w)\le N<h+\beta+e_C
 \le c^2+s-3c+2\le s^2.
\]
The last difference is $(s-c)(s+c-1)+2c-2\ge0$; the argument includes a negative $M$.

If $M\ge e_C$, choose an exact $M$-edge $u$-to-$v$ walk. Its label is $\gamma^M$, and extending it by the outside paths gives a full seed. Put $A=\max(h,s)$ and $B=\max(\beta,s)$. For $N\le A+B$, singletons give at most $3s-c$ pieces. Otherwise Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:guards} and the unary stable alphabet show that the chosen seed matches the whole target interval $[A,N-B)$. The two singleton boundaries and this interval use at most
\[
 A+B+1\le h+\beta+2s+1\le3s-c+1\le3s
\]
pieces.

\begin{proof}[Conclusion of the proof of Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:general}]
At lengths $1\le N<s$, use at most $s-1$ singletons. For every nonempty hull slice of length $N\ge s$, Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:guards} gives a live residue, Lemmas~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:interior}--\ref{ccc:sc:lem:outside} give a universal stripe and the bounded exterior paths, and exactly one of the three component cases just proved applies. The empty word has rank one when accepted. The empty language has $R(L)=1$ by definition. For $s=1$, every nonempty slice is a coordinate product on the available loop labels and has rank one. Thus all cases satisfy the claimed $s^2+8s$ bound.
\end{proof}

The qualitative eventual threshold and phase period can be large; neither appears in this estimate. The leading coefficient comes from robust primitive compression, the minimum cyclic class in the imprimitive case, and the joint outside-state budget.
\section{The matching leading coefficient from a length gate}\label{ccc:sc:sec:lower}

For completeness, we reprove the lower construction from the companion note (Part~IV). It establishes sharpness for the unrestricted class; it does not establish sharpness within the primitive factor-universal class.

\begin{proposition}[Part~IV, restated]\label{ccc:sc:prop:lower}
For every $s\ge4$ there is an $s$-state binary $\eps$-free NFA with universal coordinate hull and
\[
 R(L)=(s-3)^2.
\]
\end{proposition}
\noindent\wsevthree\ This is Part~IV's Theorem~\ref{ccc:qr:thm:main}, the
manuscript says so, and its proof is Part~IV's (the gate of
Section~\ref{ccc:qr:sec:gate} with two monochromatic loop states;
Lemmas~\ref{ccc:qr:lem:returns}, \ref{ccc:qr:lem:gap}
and~\ref{ccc:qr:lem:rank}), condensed; it is not reprinted. In it the
manuscript writes $H_m$ for Part~IV's semigroup $S_m=\langle m,m-1\rangle$,
$T_m$ for Part~IV's set of gate lengths $H_m$, and $L_m$ for Part~IV's
$\Lgate m$: the names $H_m$ and $S_m$ are \emph{swapped} relative to
