Part~IV. Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:alphabet} refers to this gate as ``the
$m=s-2$ state gate of Proposition~\ref{ccc:sc:prop:lower}''.


Combining Proposition~\ref{ccc:sc:prop:lower} with Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:general} gives the leading coefficient in \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:sharp}. The graph used for the length gate is classical; the rank argument uses the two extra monochromatic components to turn its last missing length into the number of necessary cuts. The result resolves the leading-coefficient question in the pinned report, without asserting literature-wide priority or an exact formula for $F_2(s)$.
\wsevthree\ At the pin, Part~II's Question~\ref{ccc:ps:q:rank} asked only for
the order of $F(s)$; the leading-coefficient question is Part~IV's
Question~\ref{ccc:qr:q:constant}, which the manuscript knew from Part~IV's
manuscript.
\section{The leading coefficient for finite alphabets}\label{ccc:sc:sec:alphabet}

The two-cycle geometry extends beyond two symbols, but it requires a common-anchor theorem for a selected family of cycles. Pairwise intersection alone would not justify the argument. We give that structural step explicitly and keep the dependence on alphabet size visible.

\begin{theorem}[Finite alphabet bound]\label{ccc:sc:thm:alphabet}
For $q\ge2$, put $C_q=\max\{8,2q-4\}$. Every $s$-state $\varepsilon$-free NFA over a $q$-letter alphabet with finite maximal rank satisfies
\[
 \boxed{R(L)\le s^2+C_qs.}
\]
For every $s\ge4$ and every $q\ge2$ there is such an NFA with $R(L)=(s-3)^2$, using every alphabet symbol. Hence
\[
 (s-3)^2\le F_q(s)\le s^2+\max\{8,2q-4\}s,
 \qquad \lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{F_q(s)}{s^2}=1
\]
for each fixed $q$. The same ratio tends to one along any integer sequence $q=q(s)\ge2$ with $q(s)=o(s)$. In particular the explicit upper bound $s^2+8s$ holds for $2\le q\le6$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{A common vertex for a small cycle family}

Work in an $n$-vertex digraph whose only elementary cycle lengths are coprime integers $j<k$, where $j>n/2$ and $j\ge3$. Choose $t<j$ elementary cycles, allowing repetitions, of which $a$ have length $j$ and $b$ length $k$.

\begin{lemma}[Uniqueness of the cycle counts]\label{ccc:sc:lem:counts}
Every decomposition of the sum of these cycles' integral edge-incidence vectors into elementary cycles has exactly $a$ cycles of length $j$ and $b$ of length $k$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Any such decomposition gives $aj+bk=Aj+Bk$ with $A,B\ge0$. Coprimality implies $A=a+hk$ and $B=b-hj$ for an integer $h$. If $h>0$, then $B<0$ because $b<j$; if $h<0$, then $A<0$ because $a<j<k$. Therefore $h=0$. Every nonnegative integral circulation decomposes into elementary cycles by successively removing a cycle from its positive support.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Small family common vertex]\label{ccc:sc:lem:common-vertex}
The $t$ selected cycles have a common vertex.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Sum their edge-incidence vectors as an Eulerian directed multigraph. At vertex $v$ the indegree and outdegree both equal $d_v$, the number of selected cycles containing $v$. If there were no common vertex, then $\Delta:=\max_v d_v\le t-1$. Add $\Delta-d_v$ new loops at each vertex, making all indegrees and outdegrees $\Delta$.

Replace every vertex by a left and a right copy and every directed edge by the corresponding bipartite edge. The resulting bipartite multigraph is $\Delta$-regular. For any left vertex subset, counting its incident edges proves Hall's condition, so a perfect matching exists; deleting it and repeating decomposes the multigraph into $\Delta$ perfect matchings. Each matching is a directed cycle cover. Its added loops are isolated one-vertex components. Every other cycle uses original edges and has at least $j>n/2$ vertices, so each cover contains at most one such cycle. Removing the added loops therefore decomposes the original circulation into at most $\Delta<t$ elementary cycles, contradicting Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:counts}.
\end{proof}

Fix a common vertex $z$. Let $U_j,U_k$ be the edge unions of the selected cycles of their respective lengths, omitting an empty group. Each nonempty union is strongly connected, since its constituent cycles contain $z$. Every elementary cycle of $U_j$ has length $j$: if a $k$-cycle existed, subtract one copy from the sum of the selected $j$-cycle circulations and decompose the nonnegative remainder, contradicting Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:counts}. Similarly $U_k$ has only length $k$. Thus their periods are $j$ and $k$, respectively.

Index the cyclic classes of each group from $z$. Every selected cycle visits each of its group's classes exactly once. Write $m_j=|V(U_j)|$, $m_k=|V(U_k)|$, and $m=|V(U_j)\cup V(U_k)|\le n$. Let $S_j,S_k$ be the vertices constituting singleton classes in the corresponding groups. Counting non-singleton classes gives
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:singleton-anchors}
 |S_j|\ge2j-m_j,\qquad |S_k|\ge2k-m_k.
\end{equation}
A vertex is on every selected cycle of one length if and only if it is a singleton-class vertex of that group's union: every cycle meets the class once, and every vertex of the union belongs to a selected cycle.

\subsection{Aligning the two class systems}

Assume both length groups are nonempty. For any vertex $v$ common to $U_j,U_k$, its class indices $p_j(v)\in\{0,\ldots,j-1\}$ and $p_k(v)\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}$ are its exact positions after $z$ on every selected cycle of the corresponding group that contains it. We claim
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:class-alignment}
 p_k(v)-p_j(v)\in\{0,k-j\}.
\end{equation}
For $v\ne z$, choose one cycle of each length containing $v$ and cross-splice their $z$-to-$v$ prefixes and $v$-to-$z$ suffixes. The two resulting nonempty closed walks have lengths $j+p_k(v)-p_j(v)$ and $k-p_k(v)+p_j(v)$. Their total is $j+k$. Decomposition and Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:counts} give exactly one $j$-cycle and one $k$-cycle in total. Each nonempty walk must supply one, proving \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:class-alignment}. For $v=z$, both indices are zero and the claim is immediate.

\begin{lemma}[Common anchor bound]\label{ccc:sc:lem:common-anchors}
If both length groups occur, the selected cycles have at least $2j+k-2n$ common vertices.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Their common vertices are exactly $S_j\cap S_k$. Consider $v\in S_j\cap V(U_k)$ not in $S_k$, and another vertex $w$ in its $k$-class. We first prove $w\notin V(U_j)$.

Otherwise \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:class-alignment} says that $p_j(v),p_j(w)$ either agree or differ in absolute value by $\delta=k-j$. Equality is impossible because the $j$-class of $v$ is a singleton. Also $0<\delta<j$, since $k\le n<2j$. A selected $j$-cycle containing $w$ also contains $v$, and in the forward order of their class indices supplies a path of length $\delta$ between them. The group $U_k$ supplies a return path of exactly $k$ edges: a shortest path between the distinct same-class vertices has positive length divisible by $k$ and at most $m_k-1\le n-1<2k$.

Concatenation gives a closed walk of length $k+\delta=2k-j$. This length is not a nonnegative combination of $j,k$. It can contain at most one $k$-term. With no $k$-term, divisibility would force $j\mid2k$, hence $j\le2$; with one, it would force $j\mid k$. Both contradict the hypotheses. Decomposing the closed walk into elementary cycles is therefore impossible, proving the claim.

Two vertices of $S_j$ cannot be in the same $k$-class, since the claim applied to one would exclude the other from $U_j$. Each vertex of $S_j\cap U_k$ not in $S_k$ can consequently be charged injectively to a vertex of $U_k\setminus U_j$ in its distinct $k$-class. The vertices of $S_j$ absent from $U_k$ are at most $|U_j\setminus U_k|$. Using \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:singleton-anchors},
\begin{align*}
 |S_j\cap S_k|
 &\ge |S_j|-|U_k\setminus U_j|-|U_j\setminus U_k|\\
 &\ge(2j-m_j)-(m-m_j)-(m-m_k)\\
 &=2j+m_k-2m\ge2j+k-2n.
\end{align*}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Compression with one branch for every symbol}

The exponent reduction preceding \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:longcycles} does not depend on alphabet size. Thus for a primitive factor-universal $q$-letter graph it leaves only the hard case $n\ge6$, $n/2<j<k\le n$, $\gcd(j,k)=1$, and $e\le jk$. Choose one monochromatic elementary cycle for each symbol. If $n\ge2q$, then $q<j$ and the preceding lemmas apply to all selected cycles at once.

First suppose all chosen cycles have length $\ell\in\{j,k\}$; let $r$ be the other length. Their union $U$ is strongly connected and has only elementary cycle length $\ell$, by Lemmas~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:common-vertex} and \ref{ccc:sc:lem:counts}. It has $\ell$ cyclic classes, of which at least $b\ge2\ell-m\ge1$ are singletons, where $m=|V(U)|\le n$. These singleton vertices lie on every selected cycle. Between consecutive such anchors, each symbol has a monochromatic path of the same length, equal to the cyclic-class distance. With only one anchor, the region is the full positive-length return traversal of length $\ell$.

In each equal-length region give its first position charge zero and its other positions charge one. A fragment may cross an anchor without a cut. Cutting only at symbol changes internal to a region allows each fragment to choose the relevant monochromatic branch independently, replacing whole regions in its complete witness even when the fragment begins or ends inside one. Hence charge plus one pays for the fragments in a segment. This is the same fixed-endpoint argument as Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:bubble-fragments}; disjoint interiors of the branches are unnecessary for valid walks.

A lap has charge $h=\ell-b\le m-\ell$. Set $\mu=h/\ell<1$. In a repeated selected $\ell$-cycle, a segment of length $x$ has charge at most $\mu x+h$: remove full periods beginning at the segment's own phase and bound its remaining proper-period charge by a complete lap. Choose an elementary $r$-cycle meeting a selected $\ell$-cycle at $z'$, and choose a singleton anchor $y$. Fixed paths $u\to z'$, $z'\to y$ along that cycle, and $y\to v$ have combined length $D\le2n+\ell-3$. Above the Frobenius threshold write
\[
 M-D=\alpha r+\beta\ell,\qquad0\le\alpha<\ell,\quad\beta\ge0,
\]
and use the complete walk formed by these connectors, the single $r$-cycle block at $z'$, and the repeated $\ell$-cycle block at $y$. If the threshold fails, $M<jk+2n-r-2<n^2+2n$, so singletons suffice.

Cover the entire target if $M\le2e$, or the two $e$-position boundary windows and a universal middle fragment otherwise. Their combined length is $T\le2e$. Their total intersection $K$ with the single $r$-block is less than $jk$. The repeated $\ell$-block meets at most two window segments, so the number of pieces is at most
\begin{align*}
 \mu T+(1-\mu)K+D+2h+3
 &\le jk(1+\mu)+D+2h+3\\
 &=r(\ell+h)+D+2h+3\\
 &\le rm+D+2h+3\le n^2+4n.
\end{align*}
For the last estimate use $r,m\le n$, $D\le2n+\ell-3$, and $h\le n-\ell$.

Now suppose both cycle lengths occur. Let $b$ be the number of vertices common to all selected cycles. Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:common-anchors} gives $b\ge2j+k-2n$. If $b\le2$, then $2j+k\le2n+2$ and the elementary bound suffices:
\[
 2e+1\le2jk+1\le4j(n+1-j)+1\le(n+1)^2+1\le n^2+8n.
\]
If $b\ge3$, the pairwise order lemma puts all anchors in the same cyclic order on every selected cycle. Within a length group their class indices fix every anchor-to-anchor path length. Comparing one $j$-cycle with one $k$-cycle and coarsening their pairwise bubbles to these anchors shows that exactly one region has unequal lengths, differing by $k-j$. All other regions offer an equal-length monochromatic branch for every symbol. This asserts only a property of the selected paths, not a global bubble decomposition of the whole graph.

Charge the equal regions as before. Charge every position of the unequal region one, with an additional unit on its first position. This pays for its singleton fragments and the necessary interfaces. A segment again needs at most its charge plus one pieces. The two lap charges are
\[
 c_j=j-b+2,\qquad c_k=k-b+2,
 \qquad \mu=\frac{k-b+2}{k}\le1.
\]
Their mean charges are at most $\mu$. A segment of length $x$ in either repeated cycle has charge at most $\mu x+n$, since its residual proper-period charge is at most one lap, which is at most $n$.

Choose an anchor $y$ and fixed entry and exit paths of total length $D\le2n-2$. Above the Frobenius threshold, use $M-D=\alpha j+\beta k$ and the complete ambient walk with a $j$-block followed by a $k$-block at $y$. Below the threshold, singletons give fewer than $n^2+2n$ pieces. The two boundary windows meet these two consecutive repeated-cycle blocks in at most three nonempty periodic segments. That count is used for the residual-charge overhead $3n$. The junction of the blocks is an anchor, so it adds no fragment cut: the combined repeated-cycle block meets the windows in at most two pieces of charge-plus-one bookkeeping. Including connectors and the possible middle fragment gives
\begin{align*}
 2\mu e+3n+D+3
 &\le2j(k-b+2)+5n+1\\
 &\le4j(n-j+1)+5n+1\\
 &\le n^2+7n+2\le n^2+8n.
\end{align*}
This proves the robust primitive bound $n^2+8n$ for $n\ge2q$.

For $n<2q$, use the general exponent bound $2e+1\le2(n-1)^2+3$. For $q\ge3$ and $2\le n\le2q-1$,
\[
 n^2+(2q-4)n-\bigl(2(n-1)^2+3\bigr)=n(2q-n)-5\ge0.
\]
The concave quadratic is minimized at an interval endpoint, where it is at least $2q-6\ge0$. The one-state case has rank one, and $q=2$ is covered by Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:primitive}. We have therefore proved the robust primitive bound $n^2+C_qn$ for every $n$.

\subsection{Transfer and the lower construction}

The proofs of the stable-word, universal-component, stripe, guard, entry--exit, and imprimitive-component results in Sections~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:periodic}--\ref{ccc:sc:sec:transfer} work over any finite alphabet: replace $\{0,1\}$ in the stable-alphabet definition by $\Sigma$. The imprimitive estimate $s^2+6s-3$ is within the desired bound because $C_q\ge8$.

For a primitive universal component of size $c$, let $B$ be its nonempty internal alphabet and let $h+\beta\le s-c$ be the fixed exterior-path budget. Over $B$, the robust bound is at most $c^2+C_qc$, since $C_{|B|}\le C_q$ when $|B|\ge2$. For $|B|=1$ the same estimate holds directly: a target has the unique unary label, and an exact internal witness exists for lengths at least the exponent, while shorter lengths cost at most $(c-1)^2+1$ singletons.

If $c=s$, all seed symbols lie in $B$, so apply that robust bound directly. If $c<s$, put $A=\max(h,s)$ and $Z=\max(\beta,s)$. When $N\le A+Z$, use singletons. Otherwise project $w[h:N-\beta]$ to $B$ by retaining its symbols in $B$ and replacing the others by a fixed symbol in $B$. Apply the robust primitive theorem with the required fixed endpoints and full internal length. Restrict the resulting partition to the contiguous global interval $[A,N-Z)$; its piece count does not increase. By Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:guards}, the original target already lies in $B$ there. Thus all retained internal witnesses match the actual target and extend by the fixed exterior paths to accepted length-$N$ seeds. Licensed and outside singletons use their global hull witnesses. The total cost is at most
\[
 h+\beta+2s+c^2+C_qc\le s-c+2s+c^2+C_qc\le s^2+C_qs.
\]
Indeed the last slack is $(s-c)(s+c+C_q-1)-2s\ge C_q-2\ge0$ for $c\le s-1$. Short lengths, the empty word, and the empty hull are handled as in the binary proof. This establishes the upper bound of Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:alphabet}.

For the lower bound, keep the $m=s-2$ state gate of Proposition~\ref{ccc:sc:prop:lower} and label every gate edge by all symbols. Partition $\Sigma=A\sqcup B$ into two nonempty classes. Give one extra isolated state all loop labels in $A$ and the other all loop labels in $B$, with both states initial and final as before. At a gate length every word is accepted. At a missing gate length $N$, the seeds are exactly $A^N\cup B^N$, so the hull is $\Sigma^N$ and an interval is admissible exactly when its symbols belong to one class. Rank is therefore the number of class runs. Alternating one symbol from each class at the last exceptional length $(m-1)^2$ proves exact rank $(s-3)^2$. The quantitative sandwich implies both asymptotic statements.

No alphabet-independent additive constant is established here. When $q$ is comparable to $s$, the displayed upper bound alone does not give a leading coefficient of one; for unrestricted alphabet growth that question remains open in this article.

\section{A sharp linear family and its slice maxima}\label{ccc:sc:sec:linear}

The quadratic length-gate family is asymptotically extremal, but is not the best lower bound for small state counts. A different construction attains the general exponent estimate $2e+1$ with a linear exponent, and supplies the lower witnesses for the three- and four-state extrema below.

\begin{proposition}[Shortest access and exit gates]\label{ccc:sc:prop:linear}
For every $s\ge3$ there is a primitive factor-universal binary $s$-state NFA with sole initial state $u$, sole final state $v$, exponent $e=s-1$, and
\[
 R(L)=2s-1=2e+1.
\]
More precisely, for every $N\ge1$ its slice maximum is
\[
 M_L(N):=\max\{\rank_L(w):w\in\rec(L)_N\}
       =\min\{N,2s-1\},
\]
and $0^N$ attains that maximum. Its empty word is not accepted.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Use distinct states $u,v,b$, and put a $1$-loop at every state. The only $0$-edges are $u\to v$ and $b\to b$. For $s=3$, add $1$-edges $v\to b$ and $b\to u$. For $s\ge4$, introduce states $x_1,\ldots,x_{s-3}$ and add precisely these $1$-edges:
\[
 v\to x_1,\quad b\to x_1,\quad
 x_i\to x_{i+1}\ (1\le i<s-3),\quad
 x_{s-3}\to u,\quad x_{s-3}\to b.
\]
There are no further edges. Take $I=\{u\}$ and $F=\{v\}$.

The underlying graph is strongly connected. Every walk from $u$ to $b$ must use $u\to v$ and then the full corridor; every walk from $b$ to $v$ must traverse the full corridor to $u$ and then use $u\to v$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:gate-distances}
 \operatorname{dist}(u,b)=\operatorname{dist}(b,v)=s-1.
\end{equation}
For $s=3$ this is immediate from its directed three-cycle. Every ordered pair is connected by a simple path of at most $s-1$ edges. The loops pad it to exactly $s-1$ edges, and \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:gate-distances} rules out a smaller exponent. Hence $e=s-1$. The two loops at $b$ establish factor universality, so the central-fragment argument gives $R(L)\le2s-1$.

At each zero-based coordinate $i$ of a positive length $N$, the seed $1^i01^{N-i-1}$ is accepted: wait at $u$, use $u\to v$, and wait at $v$. Consequently $0^N$ belongs to the hull. Any fragment of the target $0^N$ of length at least two must use only the $0$-loop at $b$, since the other $0$-edge ends at $v$ and no $0$-edge leaves $v$. By \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:gate-distances}, such a fragment can begin only at a boundary at least $s-1$ and end only at a boundary at most $N-s+1$.

If $N<2s-1$, no nonsingleton zero fragment fits, so $\rank_L(0^N)=N$. If $N\ge2s-1$, the first and last $s-1$ positions each force singleton pieces, and the remaining nonempty middle requires at least one piece. Thus $\rank_L(0^N)\ge2s-1$. The singleton bound and the established global upper bound give equality in both cases, proving the slice formula.
\end{proof}

This also identifies the exact maximum within the useful universal-loop class. If an $s$-state NFA has a state carrying every symbol loop and lying on an accepting walk, choose shortest access and exit paths of lengths $a,b'\le s-1$. At length $N>a+b'$, the target's middle interval $[a,N-b')$ is read at that state; its witness is completed by the two fixed paths. The outside positions are hull singletons. Shorter targets use only singletons. Hence $R(L)\le a+b'+1\le2s-1$. Proposition~\ref{ccc:sc:prop:linear} attains this for each $s\ge3$. This observation explains a limitation of searches that always insert a universal-loop state: they cannot discover superlinear rank in the full primitive class.

For binary NFAs the two ordinary lower constructions together give
\[
 F_2(s)\ge\max\{2s-1,(s-3)^2\}\qquad(s\ge4).
\]
Neither construction alone is asserted to give the exact general extremum.

\section{An exact algorithm for the maximal rank}\label{ccc:sc:sec:exact}

The asymptotic state bound and the exact value for one automaton are different questions. We now give an effective exact algorithm for any fixed finite alphabet, together with a complete finite witness-length cutoff. No guessed experimental horizon is used.

\subsection{The greedy parser is optimal}

\wsevthree\ Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:greedy}, the parser after it and the
periodic-middle graph below are Part~V's Lemma~\ref{ccc:rs:lem:greedy}, its
parser of Section~\ref{ccc:rs:sec:parser} and its reset graph
$G_{r,a_0}$ of Section~\ref{ccc:rs:sec:graph}, which the manuscript cites
elsewhere but not here. The lemma is printed as a restatement with the
manuscript's proof as a second proof; the parser is the same procedure. What
is new here is the treatment of the two boundaries, $A_r$ and $D_r$, the
positive-cycle test and the cutoff.

Fix a nonempty hull slice of length $N$ and put $S_i=P_i\cap R_{N-i}$. An interval is admissible exactly when its label can be read from $S_i$ to $S_j$ through the intervening viable layers; this is equivalent to \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:fragment-test}. In particular, restriction of an admissible interval is admissible, and every singleton of a hull word is admissible.

\begin{lemma}[Greedy optimality; Part~V, restated]\label{ccc:sc:lem:greedy}
Partition a nonempty hull word by repeatedly taking its longest admissible prefix from the current boundary. This partition has minimum size.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Second proof (manuscript~21)]
Let $g_k$ be the greedy boundaries and let $q_k$ be those of any comparison partition. Inductively $g_k\ge q_k$ as long as the greedy parser has not already finished. If $g_{k-1}\ge q_k$, the claim is immediate. Otherwise the induction hypothesis gives $q_{k-1}\le g_{k-1}<q_k$, so $[g_{k-1},q_k)$ is a restriction of the comparison partition's $k$th admissible interval. The longest greedy extension reaches at least $q_k$. Thus the greedy parser finishes in no more pieces than any comparison partition.
\end{proof}

The parser stores the nonempty subset $Z$ of possible endpoints of its current fragment. Initially $Z=S_0$ and its rank counter is $1$. At zero-based coordinate $i$, on letter $a$, compute
\[
 Z'=\Delta_a(Z)\cap S_{i+1}.
\]
If this is nonempty, replace $Z$ by $Z'$. Otherwise cut immediately before that letter, add one to the rank counter, and restart with
\[
 Z=\Delta_a(S_i)\cap S_{i+1},
\]
which is nonempty by coordinate admissibility. The subset is exactly the possible endpoints of the current greedy fragment. A failed extension cannot be rescued by later letters, so this procedure implements Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:greedy}. A nonempty target therefore has rank $1$ plus its reset count. Taking maxima of this deterministic parser output does not interchange a minimum and a maximum.

At a single length $N$, dynamic programming keeps for each current nonempty subset the largest rank counter attained there and advances over all coordinate-permitted letters. This computes $M_L(N)$ without enumerating target words; a maximizing target is recovered by retaining predecessor choices.

\subsection{Finite boundary operators and the periodic middle}

Choose any common eventual transient $t\ge0$ and period $p\ge1$ for $(P_i,R_i)$. In this section it is unnecessary to enlarge $p$ to a multiple of all cycle lengths. They can be found by following the deterministic orbit of pairs of subsets until its first repetition. Stable phase values are extended periodically to all integer phase arguments.

For a live total residue $r$, every $N\ge2t$ with $N\equiv r\pmod p$ has the following viable layers, independent of the particular $N$:
\[
 \begin{array}{ll}
 \text{left boundary:}&P_i\cap R_{r-i}^{\infty}\quad(0\le i\le t),\\[2pt]
 \text{middle phase:}&S_{r,a}=P_a^{\infty}\cap R_{r-a}^{\infty},\\[2pt]
 \text{right boundary:}&P_{r-t+j}^{\infty}\cap R_{t-j}\quad(0\le j\le t).
 \end{array}
\]
The descriptions agree at the joins. A coordinate alphabet consists exactly of letters with an edge between its two consecutive viable layers. All such alphabets are nonempty on a live slice.

Run the parser on all coordinate-permitted $t$-letter prefixes, retaining for each terminal subset $Z$ the largest rank-so-far $A_r(Z)$. This subset is at phase $t\bmod p$. When $t=0$, the initial value $1$ is assigned to the initial viable subset. Similarly, for each nonempty subset at phase $r-t\bmod p$, let $D_r(Z)$ be the largest additional reset count in the final $t$ coordinates. These finite operators can be computed by the same subset dynamic programming. Every eligible nonempty $Z$ can process the suffix, using resets if needed, so each $D_r(Z)$ is finite. Ineligible or unattainable values are $-\infty$.

The periodic-middle graph has vertices $(a,Z)$, where $a\in\mathbb Z/p\mathbb Z$ and $\varnothing\ne Z\subseteq S_{r,a}$. For each $c\in\Gamma_{r,a}$, advance to phase $a+1$ and put a directed edge of weight
\[
 \begin{cases}
 0,&\text{with target }\Delta_c(Z)\cap S_{r,a+1},\quad
                   \text{if that set is nonempty},\\
 1,&\text{with target }\Delta_c(S_{r,a})\cap S_{r,a+1},\quad
                   \text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Initialize the phase-$t$ vertices with their $A_r$ values and discard unreachable vertices.
\wsevthree\ With $t\bmod p$ as starting phase this is Part~V's graph
$G_{r,t\bmod p}$, whose vertex bound $p(2^s-1)$ is the same. Every retained vertex has an outgoing edge, and the graph has at most
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:parser-vertices}
 V=p(2^s-1)
\end{equation}
vertices.

A complete word of length $N\ge2t$ in residue $r$ is exactly a permitted prefix, a middle path of length $N-2t$ ending at phase $r-t$, and a permitted suffix. Its rank is the prefix value plus the path weight plus its suffix resets. Conversely every such combination gives a hull word, because a coordinate hull independently permits each of its coordinate letters. The terminal phase enforces the required congruence on the total length; there is no additional hidden congruence restriction.

\begin{theorem}[Exact graph algorithm]\label{ccc:sc:thm:exact-algorithm}
The maximal rank is unbounded if and only if one of the reachable periodic-middle graphs contains a directed cycle of positive total weight. Otherwise, contracting its strongly connected components and optimizing on the resulting directed acyclic graph computes the exact maximal rank over all sufficiently long slices. Together with the slices of lengths less than $2t$ and the empty-word convention, this computes $R(L)$ exactly.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Every reachable middle vertex can reach a vertex at phase $r-t$ by following arbitrary outgoing edges for the appropriate number of steps, chosen below $p$. The suffix can then be processed. Thus every reachable directed cycle is co-accessible to a complete word. A positive-weight cycle may be repeated between fixed access and exit paths. Its length is a multiple of $p$, so these complete words retain the same residue and boundary operators; their ranks grow without bound.

Conversely, if no reachable positive-weight cycle exists, every edge within a reachable strongly connected component has weight zero: a positive edge there could be completed to a positive cycle, since all weights are nonnegative. Contract these components. At a contracted vertex retain the maximum of the initial values $A_r$ and, separately, the maximum eligible terminal value $D_r$ among its members. Arbitrary entry and exit vertices in one component can be connected at zero cost. These maxima are therefore compatible with any incoming and outgoing component edges, even when attained at different original vertices. A longest-path dynamic program in the condensation graph gives exactly the largest sum of initial, transition, and terminal values. Its paths correspond to finite original paths, and hence to complete hull words as above. The finitely many short slices and the accepted empty word finish the calculation. If the hull is empty our definition gives $R(L)=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Complete finite witness cutoff]\label{ccc:sc:thm:cutoff}
If $R(L)<\infty$, every hull word of length $N\ge2t$ has the same rank as a hull word in the same total residue of length at most
\[
 2t+p(2^s-1)-1.
\]
Consequently, if the hull is nonempty, its maximal rank is attained at some length at most
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:cutoff}
 H=\max\{1,\,2t+p(2^s-1)-1\},
\end{equation}
with length zero included when the empty word is accepted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Take the target's middle parser path. By Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:exact-algorithm}, all its directed cycles have weight zero. Repeatedly delete closed portions between repeated vertices. The resulting simple path has at most $V-1$ edges and the same endpoints and total weight. Each removed length is divisible by $p$, so the terminal and total phases are unchanged. Keep the original $t$-letter prefix and suffix and the remaining middle edge labels. At the new total length all boundary layers are unchanged and each retained middle letter is still coordinate-permitted. This reconstructs a hull word with the same prefix parser value, middle reset count, final subset, and suffix reset count. Its rank is unchanged and its length is at most $2t+V-1$.

Shorter words already satisfy the bound, since $V\ge1$. The artificial lower bound $H\ge1$ only avoids a zero positive-length horizon in trivial cases. For an empty hull, the value $R(L)=1$ is a convention and is not asserted to have a witness.
\end{proof}
\wsevthree\ This complements Part~II's
Corollary~\ref{ccc:ps:cor:witness-length}, which bounds the length of
high-rank witnesses when stabilization fails, and bears on Part~II's
Question~\ref{ccc:ps:q:length}; neither is the other.

An alternative exact implementation first decides boundedness using stable-word universality and then computes all slice maxima up to $H$. Universality is decidable by the finite phase/subset graph that starts from each entire viable layer and checks whether a permitted word can reach the empty set.

\begin{corollary}[A transient bound for finite rank; Part~II, restated]\label{ccc:sc:cor:transient}
For any common eventual transient $t$ of $(P_i,R_i)$,
\[
 R(L)<\infty\quad\Longrightarrow\quad R(L)\le2t+1.
\]
\end{corollary}
\noindent\wsevthree\ This is the bound~\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:transient-bound} of
Part~II's Theorem~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:criterion}, which holds for any $t,p$
satisfying~\eqref{ccc:ps:eq:periodicity}; Part~V uses it the same way
(Corollary~\ref{ccc:rs:cor:upper}). The manuscript's proof is Part~II's
(one central fragment and $2t$ singleton boundaries) and is not reprinted.

This equivalence also proves the boundedness test just described: failure of stable-word universality yields unbounded ranks by the obstruction repetition, and success yields the transient bound. Although $t$ is useful for exact computation and pruning, it need not be a sharp state-count parameter; the compression theorem avoids paying for a potentially large transient twice.

\section{Computer assisted binary extrema through four states}\label{ccc:sc:sec:census}

Here $F_2(s)$ is the binary extremal function. Its argument counts states; it is not the alphabet index. All upper census conclusions here concern exactly two symbols. The exact algorithms of Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:exact} apply to other fixed finite alphabets, but these binary censuses do not determine their extrema.

\begin{theorem}[Exact small binary extrema]\label{ccc:sc:thm:small}
For binary $\varepsilon$-free NFAs with at most $s$ states,
\[
 \boxed{F_2(1)=1,\qquad F_2(2)=2,\qquad F_2(3)=5,\qquad F_2(4)=7.}
\]
The upper bounds for two and three states are established by exhaustive finite computation with independent implementations. The four-state upper bound combines Corollary~\ref{ccc:sc:cor:transient} with an exhaustive pruned computation. The lower bounds have explicit witnesses.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{One to three states}

For one state every nonempty accepted slice is the full product of its loop alphabet, so the maximal rank is one. For two states, use initial state $v$ and final state $u$, $1$-loops at both states, and $0$-edges $v\to v$ and $v\to u$, with no other edges. This accepts exactly the binary words containing at least one $0$: switch to $u$ at the last zero. A seed has rank one. At a length $N\ge2$, the only nonseed hull target is $1^N$, and it has rank two: split it into two nonempty intervals and place a zero outside each interval in its witnessing seed. It is not itself a seed. At length one, only $0$ belongs to the hull. Thus this NFA has $R(L)=2$, attained by $11$.

For three states Proposition~\ref{ccc:sc:prop:linear} already gives rank five with the target $0^5$. The census supplies the upper bound.

There are $2^{2s^2}(2^s-1)^2$ labeled binary transition pairs with nonempty initial and final sets. Presentations with an empty endpoint set recognize the empty language and cannot raise $F_2(s)$. The complete two- and three-state distributions are shown below. Here ``empty'' denotes an empty hull, recorded as raw maximum $0$ by the programs; under this article's floor convention it has $R(L)=1$. Every numbered rank column excludes those empty presentations.
\[
\begin{array}{c|r|r}
\text{outcome}&s=2&s=3\\\hline
\text{empty}&128&270{,}336\\
R=1&1{,}612&8{,}680{,}660\\
R=2&188&2{,}239{,}104\\
R=3&0&111{,}984\\
R=4&0&2{,}016\\
R=5&0&72\\
R=\infty&376&1{,}540{,}884\\\hline
\text{total}&2{,}304&12{,}845{,}056
\end{array}
\]

One implementation quotients transition pairs by state permutations and exchange of the binary letters, enumerates all nonempty endpoint sets, and weights each result by its exact transition-pair orbit size. For $s=3$ there are $22{,}284$ transition-pair representatives. It uses the complete cutoff and slice dynamic programming. A second implementation uses full Boolean-matrix eventual periodicity, exact boundary operators, positive-cycle detection, and periodic-middle path optimization. A third, independently written implementation directly enumerates every labeled transition pair and endpoint choice, using the paired-layer orbit and explicit boundary/core graph with strongly connected components. All three agree pointwise on all $12{,}847{,}360$ two- and three-state presentations.

The finite cutoff is at most $6$ in the finite two-state cases and at most $20$ in the finite three-state cases. An independent literal oracle enumerates accepted seeds and their actual aligned substrings, then minimizes over all interval partitions. It corroborates the parser on $208{,}712$ two-state hull targets and $147{,}264$ targets from all $72$ three-state rank-five maximizers. These cross-checks are additional evidence, not a replacement for the complete enumeration.

\subsection{A complete four-state pruning rule}

A brute-force full rank distribution is unnecessary for the four-state maximum. For each underlying relation $E$ and nonempty $I,F$, let $t$ be the least transient of the paired orbit
\[
 (P_0,R_0)=(I,F),\qquad
 (P_{i+1},R_{i+1})=(E(P_i),E^{\mathsf T}(R_i)).
\]
This orbit depends only on $E,I,F$, before the present edges are labeled. By Corollary~\ref{ccc:sc:cor:transient}, any finite-rank counterexample to $R(L)\le7$ must have $t\ge4$. All labelings of every discarded pair with $t\le3$ are therefore covered by an ordinary proof.

Enumerate the $2^{16}$ four-state underlying relations, including loops, up to state permutation. There are $3{,}044$ graph representatives. For each one, examine all $15^2$ nonempty ordered endpoint-set pairs and retain exactly those with $t\ge4$. This leaves $411$ graphs and $23{,}228$ triples $(E,I,F)$.

For each retained graph with $m$ present edges, enumerate its $3^m$ assignments of each edge as $0$-only, $1$-only, or both. These are exactly the binary transition pairs with union $E$. Retain one from each exchange of the two letters, using their integer matrix encodings to impose $\Delta_0\le\Delta_1$. Exactly one labeling is fixed by that exchange, namely the all-doubly-labeled graph. Thus each endpoint pair contributes $(3^m+1)/2$ cases. The total is independently predicted as
\[
 \frac{91{,}368{,}216+23{,}228}{2}=45{,}695{,}722.
\]

Every possible counterexample is covered: move its underlying graph to the chosen state-permutation representative and transport its labels and endpoint sets by the same permutation. All those endpoint sets and labelings are examined; exchange of the letter names preserves rank. It is unnecessary to quotient endpoint sets by graph stabilizers, since duplicate isomorphic cases do not affect a maximum. No trimming, connectedness, primitivity, or singleton-endpoint assumption is imposed.

The complete retained scan has the following outcome. These are unweighted counts inside the pruned graph-representative and letter-exchange search, \emph{not} a distribution over all labeled four-state NFAs.
\[
\begin{array}{c|r@{\qquad}c|r}
\text{outcome}&\text{count}&\text{outcome}&\text{count}\\\hline
\text{empty}&186{,}704&R=1&14{,}622{,}000\\
R=2&15{,}145{,}667&R=3&3{,}127{,}160\\
R=4&202{,}894&R=5&3{,}837\\
R=6&80&R=\infty&12{,}407{,}380
\end{array}
\]
The counts sum to $45{,}695{,}722$ and the largest finite rank in the retained class is $6$. In particular there is no retained counterexample to rank at most $7$. The discarded cases already satisfy that bound, and Proposition~\ref{ccc:sc:prop:linear} attains $7$ with four states. Thus $F_2(4)=7$. Padding with isolated states makes the conclusion valid for the at-most-state convention.

An independent candidate generator uses orbit marking and Floyd cycle detection, rather than the producer's minimum-under-permutations test and indexed orbit table. It regenerates the same $23{,}228$-record candidate list. Its independently written label enumeration and boundary/core graph rank engine completes all $45{,}695{,}722$ retained cases and reproduces every displayed count. Finite inputs are checked with the complete graph optimization; unboundedness is tested by the proved interior-universality criterion, with $12{,}492$ deterministic unbounded samples additionally checked by the full graph. All paired orbits have $t\le10$, $p\le4$, and cutoff expression $2t+15p-1\le59$; retained graphs have at most ten edges. These bounds also validate the finite arrays and integer encodings used by the implementations.

The exact small-state conclusions are computer-assisted mathematical results with compact receipts and independently reproducible source. They are not proof-assistant certificates, and they do not claim $F_2(s)$ for any $s\ge5$.

\section{Sharper bounds from common paths}\label{ccc:sc:sec:restricted}

\begin{theorem}\label{ccc:sc:thm:hamiltonian}
Let $L$ be recognized by an $n$-state, $\eps$-free, factor-universal binary NFA whose underlying digraph is primitive and has a Hamilton cycle. For arbitrary nonempty initial and final sets,
\[
 \boxed{R(L)\le n^2+n+1.}
\]
\end{theorem}

The common-path argument gives a smaller additive term than Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:primitive} in this restricted class.

\subsection{The central fragment and boundary compression}

For an underlying digraph with edge relation $E$, let
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 P_t&=\{q:\text{an $E$-walk of length $t$ goes from $I$ to $q$}\},\\
 R_t&=\{q:\text{an $E$-walk of length $t$ goes from $q$ to $F$}\}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
These are exact-length layers, with labels disregarded.

\begin{lemma}\label{ccc:sc:lem:central}
For a primitive factor-universal NFA of exponent $e$ with nonempty initial and final sets, one has $P_e=R_e=Q$. Every hull word $w$ of length $N>2e$ has an admissible central interval $[e,N-e)$. Consequently $R(L)\le2e+1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Since $I,F$ are nonempty, the definition of exponent gives both layer equalities. Factor universality realizes $w[e:N-e]$ as a labeled walk. Prepend a length-$e$ walk from $I$ to its first vertex and append a length-$e$ walk from its last vertex to $F$. Choose any available edge labels on the added walks. The result is a complete accepted seed matching the central interval. Singleton hull witnesses cover the two boundaries. If $1\le N\le2e$, the singleton bound suffices; the empty word has rank at most one when present.
\end{proof}

The next lemma is the quantitative step. A directed path is simple, so its vertices are distinct.

\begin{lemma}[Common path edge charge]\label{ccc:sc:lem:charge}
Suppose a labeled digraph on $n$ vertices has a directed path $H$ of $c\ge1$ edges with these properties:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Every edge of $H$ carries both letters.
\item No edge outside $H$ enters or leaves an internal vertex of $H$.
\item Give weight one to the first edge of $H$ and to each edge outside $H$, and weight zero to the other edges of $H$. Every elementary directed cycle has mean weight at most $\mu$, where $0\le\mu\le1$.
\end{enumerate}
Fix a hull target of length $N$ and any underlying accepting walk of length $N$. Every segment of this walk of length $\ell\ge1$ yields a partition of the corresponding target interval into at most
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:segment}
 \mu\ell+(1-\mu)(n-1)+1
\end{equation}
admissible pieces. An empty segment needs no pieces.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Delete elementary cycles successively from the segment, leaving a simple path of $m\le n-1$ edges. Its weight is at most $m$. The removed cycles have total weight at most $\mu(\ell-m)$, so the original segment's weight $W$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:weight}
 W\le\mu\ell+(1-\mu)m\le\mu\ell+(1-\mu)(n-1).
\end{equation}
This cycle deletion is used only to count weight; fragments remain on the original accepting walk.

Make each position on a non-$H$ edge a singleton, using its hull witness, and each nonempty maximal run of $H$ edges one piece. Each run is admissible: on that run choose the target's letters on the doubly labeled edges, and choose any labels on the other edges of the same complete accepting walk. The endpoints and total length are unchanged.

Except for a possible initial truncated run, every run enters through the first edge of $H$, because internal vertices admit no outside entry. Charge each such run to that edge. Charge each non-$H$ singleton to its own edge. The number of pieces is at most $W+1$, proving \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:segment}. This reasoning also covers $c=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{ccc:sc:cor:global}
Under the hypotheses of Lemmas~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:central} and \ref{ccc:sc:lem:charge},
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:global}
 R(L)\le2\mu e+2(1-\mu)(n-1)+3
       =2\mu(e-n+1)+2n+1.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
If $N>2e$, apply Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:charge} separately to the first and last $e$ edges of any length-$N$ accepting walk, and use the single central fragment. Such an accepting walk exists because the hull slice is nonempty. If $1\le N\le2e$, apply the lemma to the whole walk; its bound is no larger than \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:global}. The empty word is harmless. Boundary and central fragments may have different witnessing seeds.
\end{proof}

\subsection{High exponent structure and the quadratic bound}

Put $\omega_n=(n-1)^2+1$. We use the following classical facts from Kirkland, Olesky and van den Driessche \cite{KOV}: Theorem~1.1, Lemma~2.1, Theorems~2.2--2.3, and Corollary~2.4, printed pages~30--34. Their Theorem~1.1 credits the two-cycle-length result to Lewin and Vitek.

For a primitive Hamiltonian graph with $n\ge3$ and
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:threshold}
 e\ge\lfloor\omega_n/2\rfloor+2,
\end{equation}
there is a unique Hamilton cycle; the other elementary cycles have one length $j$, where $\gcd(n,j)=1$ and $n>j\ge\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil$. If $j>n/2$, label vertices $1,\ldots,n$ so the Hamilton edges descend modulo $n$, and the other edges are
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:chords}
 i\longrightarrow i+j-1\quad(i\text{ in a subset of }\{1,\ldots,a\}),\qquad
 1\le a\le n-j+1,
\end{equation}
with sources $1,a$ present. Their exact exponent formula is
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:exponent}
 e=n-a+1+(n-2)j.
\end{equation}
The exceptional possibility is odd $n$ with $j=(n-1)/2$; the same formula holds with $1\le a\le j$. These imported statements concern the underlying graph, without labels.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:hamiltonian}]
First assume $n\ge4$, \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:threshold}, and $j>n/2$. Set $c=j-a\ge0$. Consider first $c\ge1$ and the descending path
\[
 H:\quad j\longrightarrow j-1\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow a,
\]
of $c$ edges. Every elementary $j$-cycle uses one chord and contains $H$. To verify the one-chord assertion, a $j$-cycle using $r$ chords has total displacement $-j+rj$ modulo $n$, so $r\equiv1\pmod n$. Since $0\le r\le a<n$, it follows that $r=1$. Its remaining edges descend from $i+j-1$ back to its chord source $i$, and include all edges of $H$. The Hamilton cycle contains $H$ as well.

No internal vertex of $H$ has an outside entering or leaving edge. Indeed, chord sources are at most $a$, while chord targets lie between $j$ and $a+j-1\le n$. There is no modular wrap in the chord targets.

Every edge of $H$ is doubly labeled. For either letter $x$, factor universality supplies arbitrarily long $x$-labeled walks. A sufficiently long such walk contains an elementary directed cycle all of whose edges carry $x$. Every elementary cycle contains $H$, so all edges of $H$ carry $x$.

Use the weights of Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:charge}. Each $j$-cycle has total weight $a+1$, and the Hamilton cycle has total weight $n-j+a+1$. Their means obey
\[
 j(n-j+a+1)-n(a+1)=(n-j)(c-1)\ge0.
\]
Thus the maximum cycle mean is
\[
 \mu=\frac{n-j+a+1}{n}\le1.
\]
By \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:global} and \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:exponent},
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:rankparameter}
 R(L)\le\frac{2}{n}(n-j+a+1)\bigl((n-2)j-a+2\bigr)+2n+1.
\end{equation}

Put $k=n-j$ and $G(a)=(k+a+1)((n-2)j-a+2)$. On the real interval $1\le a\le k+1$,
\[
 G'(a)=(n-2)j-k+1-2a\ge(n+1)j-3n-1\ge0.
\]
The last inequality follows from $j>n/2$ when $n\ge6$, and from the integer bound $j\ge3$ when $n=4,5$. Hence $G(a)\le G(k+1)$. Substitution into \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:rankparameter} gives
\begin{align*}
 R(L)&\le\frac{4(n-1)}{n}(n-j+1)(j-1)+2n+1\\
     &\le n(n-1)+2n+1=n^2+n+1,
\end{align*}
since $(n-j+1)+(j-1)=n$. The endpoint $a=k+1$ is only an algebraic maximizer; it need not satisfy $c\ge1$. The path lemma was applied at the actual graph's parameters.

If $c=0$, then $a=j\le n-j+1$ and $j>n/2$ force odd $n$ and $a=j=(n+1)/2$. Equation~\eqref{ccc:sc:eq:exponent} gives $e=(n^2-1)/2$, so Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:central} gives $R(L)\le n^2$.

If \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:threshold} fails, then
\[
 R(L)\le2e+1\le(n-1)^2+4\le n^2+n+1\qquad(n\ge2).
\]
If the exceptional case $j=(n-1)/2$ holds, \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:exponent} and $a\ge1$ give
\[
 R(L)\le2e+1\le n^2-n+3\le n^2+n+1.
\]
These cover all $n\ge4$ cases.

For $n=1$, factor universality forces both self-loop labels, and $R(L)=1$. For $n=2,3$, the classical primitive exponent bound $e\le(n-1)^2+1$ gives $R(L)\le5,11$, respectively. That bound and these small cases are recalled in \cite[Section~1]{KOV}. They satisfy the claimed estimate.
\end{proof}

\section{Graphs with a cycle on all but one vertex}\label{ccc:sc:sec:near}

The \emph{circumference} of a digraph is the maximum length of an elementary directed cycle. The compression argument extends to the case where at most one vertex is omitted.

\begin{theorem}\label{ccc:sc:thm:near}
Let $L$ be recognized by an $n$-state, $\eps$-free, factor-universal binary NFA with primitive underlying digraph and circumference at least $n-1$. For arbitrary nonempty initial and final sets,
\[
 \boxed{R(L)\le n^2+3n.}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}[Several common paths]\label{ccc:sc:lem:paths}
In a primitive factor-universal $n$-state NFA with nonempty initial and final sets, suppose there are $t\ge1$ pairwise vertex-disjoint directed paths of positive lengths, with $C$ edges in total. Each edge carries both letters, internal path vertices have no outside incoming or outgoing edges, and every elementary cycle contains all the paths. If all cycle lengths are at most $k$, their mean weights under the first-edge charge are at most
\[
 \mu=1-\frac{C-t}{k}.
\]
The segment estimate \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:segment} and global bound \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:global} hold with this $\mu$, or with any larger valid cycle-mean bound in $[0,1]$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Charge every outside edge and the first edge of every path by one, and all other path edges by zero. A cycle of length $\ell$ has weight $\ell-(C-t)$, hence mean at most $1-(C-t)/k$. Every path run except possibly an initial truncated run contains its charged first edge. Therefore pieces are bounded by weight plus one, just as in Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:charge}. The same residual simple-path estimate proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:near}]
The Hamiltonian case follows from Theorem~\ref{ccc:sc:thm:hamiltonian}. Assume first $n\ge6$, exponent satisfying \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:threshold}, and circumference $k=n-1$. By \cite[Theorems~1.1--1.2]{KOV}, the elementary cycle lengths are $k$ and $j$, with
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:near-exp}
 \gcd(j,k)=1,\qquad j\ge\lceil n/2\rceil,\qquad e\le n+j(n-3).
\end{equation}
If $j=n/2$, Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:central} gives $R(L)\le n^2-n+1$, so assume $j>n/2$.

The classification \cite[Theorem~3.3]{KOV} places the graph, up to relabeling and reversal of all arcs, inside one of two templates. Start with the descending cycle on $1,\ldots,k$ and every chord
\[
 i\to i+j-1\qquad(1\le i\le a_0=n-j).
\]
Either add vertex $n$ with the same base predecessors and successors as one base vertex $v$, or add edges $1\to n$, $n\to n-2$, $n\to j-1$.

Reversal causes no loss: reverse all labels' edges, exchange $I$ and $F$, and reverse each word. This bijects seeds and hull words, preserves every aligned partition after reversing its interval order, and preserves factor universality. Thus $R(L)$ is unchanged.

In the base template, every elementary cycle contains the descending path $H$ from $j$ to $a_0$, of length
\[
 c=j-a_0=2j-n.
\]
The base cycles have lengths $j$ and $k$, with a unique $k$-cycle and one chord in each $j$-cycle, by the Hamiltonian classification or the displacement argument used above. Internal vertices of $H$ have unique incoming and outgoing base edges.

In the replication template, project $n$ to $v$. Every elementary cycle projects to an elementary base cycle. Otherwise the only repeated projected vertex is $v$, and splitting there gives two nonempty closed base walks. Each has length at least the base girth $j$, forcing an original elementary cycle of length at least $2j>n$, which is impossible. Remove from $H$ its at most two edges incident with $v$. The remaining edges form at most two vertex-disjoint nonempty paths after empty pieces are discarded. Every template cycle contains them, their internal vertices retain unique incoming and outgoing edges, and their total length $C$ and number $t$ satisfy
\[
 C\ge c-2,\qquad t\le2,\qquad C-t\ge c-4.
\]

In the other template, the descending path from $j-1$ to $a_0$ is common to all cycles and has $c-1$ edges. For a cycle through $n$, entrance is from $1$. Exit to $n-2$ gives a descending return path and a $k$-cycle; exit to $j-1$ gives a $j$-cycle. A chord on either return route would force a repeated base vertex before returning to $1$, so no further possibility occurs. Internal vertices of this shortened path have unique incoming and outgoing edges. For $c\ge2$ it is a positive-length path with $C-t=c-2\ge c-4$. At $c=1$ it is empty, a case handled below without compression.

Each retained edge lies in every cycle of the relevant full template, so it lies in every cycle of the actual strongly connected subgraph. In particular it is present there. Factor universality forces it to carry both letters, using a monochromatic elementary cycle for each letter as in the Hamiltonian proof. Internal degree conditions persist under deletion of edges.

For $c\ge4$, Lemma~\ref{ccc:sc:lem:paths} therefore permits the larger mean bound
\[
 \mu_0=1-\frac{c-4}{n-1}=\frac{2n-2j+3}{n-1}\in[0,1].
\]
If no positive-length path remains when $c=4$, charging every edge by one gives the same bound $\mu_0=1$ directly.

If $c\le4$, use \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:near-exp} and $j\le(n+4)/2$ to obtain
\[
 R(L)\le2e+1\le2n+(n+4)(n-3)+1=n^2+3n-11.
\]
If $c\ge4$, \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:global} and \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:near-exp} give
\[
 R(L)\le\frac{2(2n-2j+3)(j(n-3)+1)}{n-1}+2n+1.
\]
The derivative of the numerator product with respect to $j$ is
\[
 (n-3)(2n+3-4j)-2<0\qquad\bigl(j\ge(n+4)/2\bigr).
\]
Evaluate at $j=(n+4)/2$ to get $R(L)\le n^2+3n-9$. Both estimates imply the theorem.

Below the high-exponent threshold, use $R(L)\le(n-1)^2+4$. For $2\le n\le5$, the primitive exponent bound gives $R(L)\le2(n-1)^2+3\le n^2+3n$. For $n=1$ the rank is one, and an accepted empty word always has rank one. This exhausts the cases.
\end{proof}

\section{A large exponent without a total labeled state subset}\label{ccc:sc:sec:barrier}

A nonempty $S\subseteq Q$ is \emph{forward-total} if every $q\in S$ has, for each letter, an outgoing edge into $S$. It is \emph{backward-total} if every $q\in S$ has, for each letter, an incoming edge from $S$. The next family shows why such subsets cannot be assumed even near the maximum primitive exponent.

\begin{proposition}\label{ccc:sc:prop:barrier}
For every odd $n\ge5$ there is a primitive, Hamiltonian, factor-universal binary graph on $n$ vertices with exponent $(n-1)(n-2)$, no nonempty forward-total or backward-total subset, and
\[
 R(L)\le6n+3
\]
for every choice of nonempty initial and final sets.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Use $Q=\{0,\ldots,n-1\}$, Hamilton edges $i\to i-1$ modulo $n$, and chords
\[
 0\to n-3,\qquad 2\to n-1.
\]
The edges $0\to n-1$ and $1\to0$ carry only $0$; the edge $n-1\to n-2$ carries only $1$. Every other edge, including both chords, carries both letters.

Set $A=Q\setminus\{n-2\}$ and $C=Q\setminus\{0\}$. Directly,
\begin{equation}\label{ccc:sc:eq:subsets}
 \delta(S,0)=A,\qquad\delta(S,1)=C\qquad(S\in\{Q,A,C\}).
\end{equation}
For completeness, deleting $0$ can remove only targets $n-1,n-3$; alternative suppliers $2,n-2$ remain in $C$. Deleting $n-2$ can remove only target $n-3$; its alternative supplier $0$ remains in $A$. Together with the displayed full-set images this proves \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:subsets}, letter by letter. Thus every nonempty word maps $Q$ to $A$ or $C$, according to its last letter, proving factor universality.

A forward-total subset must omit $1$, which has no outgoing $1$, and $n-1$, which has no outgoing $0$. Both targets of $2$ are then absent; the unique outgoing chain excludes $3,\ldots,n-2$, and finally both targets of $0$ are absent. A backward-total subset must omit $0$, which has no incoming $1$, and $n-2$, which has no incoming $0$. The predecessors of $n-3$ are then absent; this exclusion propagates down to $1$. The two predecessors $0,2$ of $n-1$ are also absent. Both kinds of subset are therefore empty.

The elementary cycle lengths are $n$ and $n-2$. An elementary cycle cannot use both chords: after the chord from $0$, it meets $2$ before returning to $0$, and using the second chord would revisit a vertex; the reversed order is analogous. The graph is primitive since $n$ is odd. In the notation of \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:chords}, after increasing the vertex names by one, $j=n-2$ and $a=3$. The graph belongs to the class of \cite[Theorem~2.2]{KOV}, so Corollary~2.4 there gives
\[
 e=n-2+(n-2)^2=(n-1)(n-2).
\]
Its girth is $g=n-2$.

Choose any $f\in F$. There is a suffix length $b\le n-2=g$ such that $|R_b|\ge2$. Follow predecessors of $f$ along the Hamilton cycle until reaching $n-3$ or $n-1$, then take one further predecessor step; these are precisely the two vertices of indegree two. This takes at most $n-2-f$ steps if $0\le f\le n-3$, at most two if $f=n-2$, and one if $f=n-1$. The singleton final set $\{f\}$ has two predecessors at that first branch, and monotonicity gives the assertion for $F$.

If $N>e+b$, the nonempty word $w[e:N-b]$ maps $Q=P_e$ onto a set missing just one state, by \eqref{ccc:sc:eq:subsets}. That image meets $R_b$, so this entire central interval is admissible. Singleton boundaries would already give $R(L)\le e+b+1\le(n-1)^2$.

For the stronger linear estimate, fix any length-$N$ accepting underlying walk. Choose the target's symbol at every doubly labeled edge and the prescribed symbol at each other edge. This gives a seed that can disagree only at singly labeled edges. A given source vertex appears at most $\lceil\ell/g\rceil$ times among the sources of a walk segment of $\ell$ edges: two occurrences are separated by a positive closed walk, of length at least $g$. Only three source vertices, $0,1,n-1$, have singly labeled edges. Hence a segment has at most $3\lceil\ell/g\rceil$ potential disagreements. A segment with $d$ disagreements is covered by at most $2d+1$ admissible pieces: matching runs use the constructed seed, and each disagreement uses its singleton hull witness.

If $1\le N\le e+b\le ng$, this gives at most $6n+1$ pieces. If $N>e+b$, apply it to the first $e=(n-1)g$ edges and the last $b\le g$ edges, using at most $6(n-1)+1$ and $7$ pieces, respectively. Add the central piece to obtain $6n+3$. The empty-word convention completes the proof.
\end{proof}

The family has $e/n^2\to1$ but $R(L)/n^2\to0$, uniformly over the endpoint sets. Thus near-extremal exponent alone does not produce a quadratic rank lower bound. It also disproves the proposed route of finding a nonempty total original-state subset in every high-exponent factor-universal graph. Its large reachable subsets and sparse singly labeled edges instead make the rank small.

\section{Scope and reproducible checks}\label{ccc:sc:sec:scope}

The all-size theorem establishes the leading coefficient for every fixed finite alphabet, quantitatively as $F_q(s)\le s^2+\max\{8,2q-4\}s$. Together with the alphabet-independent lower family it also covers $q=o(s)$. It does not establish a leading-one estimate uniform over unrestricted alphabet growth or the optimal additive term. The binary finite function is known here exactly only through four states. The sharper Hamiltonian and near-Hamiltonian estimates do not have matching lower constructions in those classes. A near-maximal primitive exponent can coexist with linear rank and no nonempty forward-total or backward-total state subset.

The evidence has three distinct roles. First, the periodic-obstruction, component, stripe, entry--exit, bubble, circulation, common-anchor, parser, cutoff, pruning, and explicit-family arguments are ordinary proofs supplied in this article. The primitive exponent and high-exponent structural inputs are classical results cited precisely in \cite{KOV}. The length-gate construction is reproduced from the companion note (Part~IV). Second, the exact binary two-, three-, and four-state upper extrema depend on complete finite computations whose coverage is justified in Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:census}. Independent implementations reproduce the counts, with pointwise agreement for all two- and three-state presentations and a full independently regenerated pruned four-state run. Third, the finite parameter, bookkeeping, template, and family checks corroborate the all-size proofs; they are not premises in place of those proofs.

The accompanying source package contains the editable article, PDF, build script, exact implementations, reproduction scripts, compact count and audit receipts, consistency checks, pinned source descriptions, and checksums. Millions of raw automaton records and compiled executables are omitted. Candidate lists can be regenerated by the supplied programs, and their digests compared with the receipts. The README distinguishes fast receipt and consistency validation from complete exhaustive reruns.

The mathematical arguments and computation packages have undergone separate independent review, followed by review of the integrated article. This is an unrefereed research note with ordinary and computer-assisted proofs, not a Lean development or another proof-assistant certificate. The provenance claim is local: the results answer questions developed from the pinned report, without claiming first discovery throughout the literature.

\section{The classical universality input}\label{ccc:app:universality}

\wadd{Appendices~\ref{ccc:app:universality}--\ref{ccc:app:provenance}
belong to Part~I and are printed after Parts~II and~III so that no letter or
number of Part~I moved.}
\waddsevone{Part~IV is printed before them too.}
\waddsevthree{So are Parts~V--VII.}

For completeness, here is the standard route to the co-r.e.-completeness used
in Section~\ref{ccc:sec:undecidable}. The foundational undecidable problem is Post
correspondence~\cite{Post}. Take two morphisms $h,g:I^*\to A^*$ and ask whether
some $x\in I^+$ satisfies $h(x)=g(x)$. The set of positive instances is
recursively enumerable, and the usual effective computation-history reduction
gives r.e.-completeness.

Use disjoint alphabets $I,A$ and a separator $\$$. Among encodings
$x\$y^{\mathrm R}$, where $x\in I^+$ and $y\in A^*$, the language
\[
 \{x\$y^{\mathrm R}:y\ne h(x)\}
\]
is context-free. A pushdown automaton pushes the successive output blocks of
$h$ while reading $x$, then compares the reversed candidate output against the
stack. It accepts a symbol mismatch, a short candidate, or a long candidate;
the regular encoding conditions can be imposed separately. The analogous
language for $g$ is also context-free. Their union, together with all malformed
encodings, is universal exactly when the correspondence instance has no
solution. This proves co-r.e.-hardness of grammar universality. Its complement
is r.e. because grammar membership is decidable and nonmembers can be searched
for by length.

To obtain a binary alphabet, map each letter of the finite encoding alphabet
to a distinct binary block of one common length $\ell$. For a language $H$
over that alphabet, set
\[
 H'=c(H)\cup\bigl(\{0,1\}^*\setminus c(A_0^*)\bigr),
\]
where $A_0$ denotes the entire encoding alphabet. The image $c(H)$ is
context-free and $c(A_0^*)$ is regular. The injective fixed-length code gives
$H'=\{0,1\}^*$ exactly when $H=A_0^*$. This is a reduction for universality;
as Section~\ref{ccc:sec:questions} emphasizes, it is \emph{not} automatically a
reduction preserving crossover.

\section{Why the positional counts are eventually affine}
\label{ccc:app:counting}

The special case of Presburger counting needed here also has a direct
one-dimensional explanation. After quantifier elimination, a predicate in
variables $(n,i)$ is a Boolean combination of linear inequalities and fixed
modular congruences. We also impose $0\leq i<n$.

Refine the residue class of $n$ modulo all congruence moduli, and refine the
residue class of $i$ similarly. On these classes, congruences have constant
truth values. Each nontrivial inequality in $i$ changes truth value at a
rational-affine threshold in $n$. The order of finitely many such thresholds
is fixed once $n$ is sufficiently large: two affine functions either agree
identically or their order changes at most once.

Thus the allowed positions eventually form a fixed finite union of intervals
between affine thresholds, possibly with selected endpoint positions, in each
fixed residue class of $i$. Counting that class inside such an interval is a
difference of floor or ceiling functions of rational-affine expressions.
Refine the period of $n$ to absorb the finitely many denominators. Each such
count then is affine on a progression. Finite union and disjoint refinement
preserve that property.

This argument explains both effectivity and the degree-one conclusion of
Lemma~\ref{ccc:lem:affinecounts}; the general counting theorem of Woods supplies a
systematic framework. Since counts are nonnegative for all sufficiently large
parameters and integer-valued, their progression increments are nonnegative
integers. That final observation is what makes the geometric factors in
Theorem~\ref{ccc:thm:rational} polynomials rather than Laurent expressions.

contributed. The choices made were these. Part~IV answers Part~II's
Question~\ref{ccc:ps:q:rank} and imports Part~II's upper bound; it was kept
as a Part of its own, not merged into Part~II, because its construction is
new and shares no proof with Part~II. Its restatement of the rank calculus
is printed as a marked restatement with its proof as one more route
(Proposition~\ref{ccc:qr:prop:clocks}); its last-gap lemma received a
credit to Sylvester's two-generator Frobenius formula; its gate language was
renamed $\Lgate m$, because Part~II's $L_m$ is another family; its four
unnumbered questions received numbers and labels; every label received the
prefix \texttt{ccc:qr:}; its bibliography entry for the pinned report was
replaced by internal references, its To entry identified with \cite{To},
and two entries (Sylvester, Ram\'irez Alfons\'in) were added for the credit.
Dated notes were added in Part~I's
Section~\ref{ccc:sec:verification}, its conclusion and
Appendix~\ref{ccc:app:universality}, in Part~II's delivered status, after
Theorem~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:lower-family}, after
Questions~\ref{ccc:ps:q:rank} and~\ref{ccc:ps:q:formal}, in Part~II's
conclusion, and here (the title page, already full, received none); no
existing label, theorem, section, equation or
question number changed.

\paragraph{\waddsevthree{A fifth manuscript.}} On 1 October 2026 batch-73
manuscript~33, \emph{Rational Rank Slopes for Constrained Crossover}
(archive \texttt{rational-rank-slopes-package.zip}), written against
Parts~I--III at ProveIt commit \texttt{1bd730779}, which arrived in commit
\texttt{f8c3a392a} and was placed in commit \texttt{5e4f1263c}, became
Part~V. The report is now built from five manuscripts;
Section~\ref{ccc:rs:sec:source} gives this one's provenance and what it
contributed. The choices made were these. Part~V answers Part~II's
Question~\ref{ccc:ps:q:slope} and was kept as a Part of its own, because its
theorem and its proof (greedy optimality, the subset-reset graph, cycle
means) are new; its restatement of Part~II's aligned fragment criterion
became a pointer without the repeated proof, its periodic definitions and
its parity example are kept with pointers to Part~II; its slope $\lambda_r$
was renamed $\sigma_r$, because Part~III's $\lambda_v$ is another rational
quantity; its sentence of next questions became four numbered questions;
every label received the prefix \texttt{ccc:rs:}; its bibliography entries
for the pinned report and for Part~IV's manuscript were replaced by internal
references, its Manzoni entry identified with \cite{Manzoni}, and its
Karp~(1978) entry printed as \cite{KarpCycle}, because \cite{Karp} is another
paper. Dated notes were added after Questions~\ref{ccc:ps:q:rank},
\ref{ccc:ps:q:slope}, \ref{ccc:qr:q:constant} and~\ref{ccc:qr:q:dfa}, after Part~II's fixed-word
algorithm and Corollary~\ref{ccc:ps:cor:residue}, in Part~II's conclusion,
in Part~I's conclusion, in Part~IV's Section~\ref{ccc:qr:sec:scope}, in
Appendix~\ref{ccc:app:universality} and here; no existing label, theorem,
section, equation or question number changed.

\paragraph{\waddsevthree{A sixth manuscript.}} On 1 October 2026 batch-73
manuscript~29, \emph{Finite Crossover Stabilization for Deterministic
Automata} (archive \texttt{dfa-crossover-package.zip}, revised edition),
written against Parts~I--III at ProveIt commit \texttt{1bd730779}, which
arrived in commit \texttt{f8c3a392a} and was placed in commit
\texttt{26abf259b}, became Part~VI. Its earlier edition, manuscript~32
(\texttt{dfa-crossover-package~(1).zip}), was superseded and is neither
printed nor shipped. The report is now built from six manuscripts;
Section~\ref{ccc:dc:sec:source} gives this one's provenance and what it
contributed. The choices made were these. Part~VI answers Part~II's
Question~\ref{ccc:ps:q:dfa} and Part~IV's Question~\ref{ccc:qr:q:dfa} and was
kept as a Part of its own. Its two lemmas on actual alphabets and on the
joint entry and exit budget are printed here, with their proofs, once; a
later manuscript states them for NFAs with the same proofs. Its restatement
of the report's background is kept with pointers; its gate lemma, whose first
assertions are Part~IV's, keeps its proof, because its labelling and its
common completion differ. Its language $L_m$ was renamed $\Ldfa m$ and the
initial state $r$ of its gate automaton $q_{\rm in}$; a lost \verb|\rm| in
one display was restored; its sentence of open refinements became four
numbered questions; every label received the prefix \texttt{ccc:dc:}; its
bibliography entries for the pinned report and for the manuscripts of
Parts~IV and~V were replaced by internal references, its Schwarz entry
identified with \cite{Schwarz}, and two entries (Stockmeyer--Meyer,
Gawrychowski et~al.) added. Dated notes were added wherever the report
called the DFA complexity or the DFA order open: in Part~I after
Corollary~\ref{ccc:cor:regularstability} and Question~\ref{ccc:q:regular}
and in its conclusion; in Part~II's opening, delivered status, comparison
table, Section~\ref{ccc:ps:sec:dfa}, the comment after
Theorem~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:lower-family}, after
Questions~\ref{ccc:ps:q:dfa}, \ref{ccc:ps:q:rank} and~\ref{ccc:ps:q:period},
and in its conclusion; in Part~III after its complexity remark, after
Question~\ref{ccc:cl:q:explicit} and in its conclusion; in Part~IV's
opening, its list of what it leaves unaffected, after
Questions~\ref{ccc:qr:q:constant} and~\ref{ccc:qr:q:dfa} and after its
remark on the DFA decision question; in Appendix~\ref{ccc:app:universality}
and here. No existing label, theorem, section, equation or question number
changed.

\paragraph{\waddsevthree{A seventh manuscript.}} On 1 October 2026 batch-73
manuscript~21, \emph{Sharp Leading Constant for Constrained Crossover Rank}
(archive \texttt{common-path-rank-package.zip}), written against Parts~I--III
at ProveIt commit \texttt{1bd730779} and citing Part~IV's manuscript at
commit \texttt{aee32ad45}, which arrived in commit \texttt{f8c3a392a} and
was placed in commit \texttt{5e4f1263c}, became Part~VII. Its earlier
version, manuscript~24 (\texttt{common-path-rank-package~(1).zip}), was
superseded and is neither printed nor shipped. The report is now built from
seven manuscripts; Section~\ref{ccc:sc:sec:source} gives this one's
provenance and what it contributed. The choices made were these. Part~VII
answers Part~IV's Question~\ref{ccc:qr:q:constant} as to the leading constant
and was kept as a Part of its own. It shares two lemmas, with the same
proofs, with Part~VI; they are printed once, with their proofs, in Part~VI,
and Part~VII states them for NFAs and points there. Its reproofs of Part~II's
criterion and transient bound and of Part~IV's construction became pointers
without the repeated proofs; its greedy lemma, Part~V's, keeps its proof as a
second proof. Its backward layers $B_j$ were renamed $R_j$, Part~II's name;
four sections without labels received labels; every label received the
prefix \texttt{ccc:sc:}; its bibliography entries for the pinned report and
for the manuscripts of Parts~IV and~V were replaced by internal references,
its Schwarz entry (not cited in its text) identified with \cite{Schwarz}, and
one entry (Kirkland--Olesky--van den Driessche) added. Two of its delivered
files are Part~IV's shipped checker and record and were not shipped again.
Dated notes were added wherever the report called the leading constant open
or bounded it by two: after Questions~\ref{ccc:ps:q:rank}
and~\ref{ccc:qr:q:constant}, after the comment on
Theorem~\ref{ccc:ps:thm:lower-family}, in Part~II's delivered status and
conclusion, in Part~IV's opening, its list of answers, its single-initial-state
paragraph and Section~\ref{ccc:qr:sec:scope}, in Part~V after
Corollary~\ref{ccc:rs:cor:upper} and in its list of what it leaves open, and in
Part~VI after Theorem~\ref{ccc:dc:thm:leading-one}, after its two shared
lemmas, at the end of Section~\ref{ccc:dc:sec:leading-one} and in its list of
what it leaves open; also in Part~I's conclusion, in
Appendix~\ref{ccc:app:universality} and here. No existing label, theorem,
section, equation or question number changed.
